Sass an Expert. TO BEGIN AT £lO COST.

I have often been asked which is the best time to start poultry-keeping with a view to making the maximum profits, writes an expert in "Farm and home." It is a question somewhat difficult to answer, because a great deal depends on the man at the wheel, on the conditions under which he intends to keep fowls, on the breeds selected, and the amount of capital at his disposal. Poultry-keeping is not something which runs away with a lot of money, unless It is attempted on a big scale; but it is neces- sary to have at least £lO to make even a small start. It is not often a man who commences with poor quality, perhaps second-hand appli- ances, where the houses are simply worthless when purchased, makes much money; he is generally confronted with twice as much disease as there should be, the winter egg supply is far from satisfactory, and when the chicken-breed- ing season comes round, numerous. calamities seetn to happen. Personally I like the idea of Making a start In August or September_ For one thing the weather is favourable, outdoor work- is most congenial, a man Can spend any part of the day, doing a few odd jobs, and thoroughly enjoy it ; but in the early spring months it is•exactly the opposite, most days are cold and wet, and out- door work is disagreeable, unless one is accus- tomed to it. But it is more important from the financial standpoint that a start in August or September should be made. novice should never begin in a big way—it will mean.the loss of much money' he should feel his way and launch out gradually.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
REFUSE DEFORMED BIRDS.

For a man -;11—o starts in September, there are two courses open. When the yard has been got into proper order, either, this year's pullets, well-grown, early-hatched birds, can be pur- chased, or some yearling hens. It all depends on the amount of morrey which he can afford L o spend. Pullets will cost anything from ss. each upwards--that is decent utility stuff liy kel to be of service later. Deformed birds and un- healthy stock should be absolutely refused. no matter how low the price. I firmly believe that If a lot of March pullets can be had in August or September, these with ordinary care and good feeding should start laying during the first or second .week of November, and, given moderate weather, will produce enough eggs fiefore Christmas to repay their original cost. This is good business, and it is exactly what should be attempted by every poultry-keeper in the land ; whether he buys pullets or breeds them, they should almost have cleared them- selves at the end of the year. When this has been done, there is a future of great promise.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
_ SALT FOR PRESERVING EGGS.

When eggs are to be preserved for not more than three months dry salt answers the pur- pose admirably. -This method, however, is suitable, only when the. eggs are required for home use, since the salt absorbes a certain amount of motsture from the egg, causing. its contents to rattle somewhat, and giving the purchaser an impression of staleness. A layer of salt should be placed in the bottom of a box, then a layer of eggs, another layer of salt, and SQ on, until the box is full, when the lid should be screwed on, so that when the eggs are wanted for use it can be removed and the stale eggs used first. Smearing the shells with lamp- black or soot preserves the contents fairly well, but makes the eggs valueless for marketing purposes.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DUST BATH.

IL is surprising to find how few poultry keepers realise the importance of a dust bath for their birds, in order that they may keep themselves free from insect pests. The provi- sion of a well-filled dust bath is not an expen- e.ve matter, and it should be kept in a con- venient spot in every poultry yard. Any good- sized grocer's box will do, whilst if raised about a foot from the ground, by nailing four legs on it, and placed in the sunniest spot of the poul- try run, it will be much appreciated by birds of all ages. Dust boxes should not be fixtures, but movable, with handles at side or at top. The best dust bath will be found in a dry mix- ture of road dust 20 parts, sulphur 2 parts, lime 3 parts, pyrethrum powder 1 part. This is usually effective enough for most purposes, but may be increased in strength if .necessary.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
LATE-HATCHED PULLETS.

The late-hatched pullets—April to May birds —need every care and attention that can be given ttlem. It is a fallacy of most poultry- keepers 'that a little forcing in late autumn is all that is needed to bring these late-hatched chickens along and' turn them into as good layers and as good breeding stock as their earlier hatched sisters. The effect of this forc- ing frequently is to induce laying, but the re- sulting eggs are invariably small, and produc- tivity is relatively decreased, while the progeny of these birds are weak and under-sized.

The late pullets need bringing along from the moment they are hatched to the moment they go into winter quarters. Every step of the road must be taken carefully. There must be no hanging fire in development, even for a day, no leaving them to look after themselves, no trust- ing in a beneficent Providence. Housing must be perfect; feeding must be perfect. From the earliest days all grain must be given in deep litter of one sort or another, and meat meal should form a part of all the soft food rations.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
SELLING OLD HENS.

When once hens commence to moult, their flesh is in very, poor condition, and if they are retained until they have coMpleted the process, much time is wasted, and a quantity of food is used that otherwise would be saved were the birds disposed of when they cease laying. When the district is one in which game abounds, there Is very little difficulty in disposing of the old hens to keepers, who will usually pay a good price for them. I know, says an expert, of vil- lages in close proximity to ,game preserves where the local poultry-keepers regard the sale of their two-year-old hens as quite a large item in their z annual poultry profits. The prices for thefl hens vary considerably. I have known as niuch as 4s. paid, while I have come across cases where as little as is. 3d. was offered. In some counties it is the eustom for poultry-keepers and gamekeepers to fix a sum for the loan of hens until the end of the game-rearing season. This is an excellent plan when satisfactory arrangements can be made, especially so when the hens are to be kept for another season's laying, since the gamekeeper has them over a period when they are not lay- ing, and they are returned to the poultry-keeper In good condition and fortified for the winter's work, and undoubtedly they make the best winter layers. "

ONLY GOOD WEATHER NECESSARY TO MAKE A PROSPEROUS YEAR.

The harvest is now general in this part of Lancashire and Cheshire, arid, provided the weather keeps favourable, it is likely to prove one of the best seasons, as' regards cereal crops, farmers have had for a few years past. W-I'teat, barley and oats have headed well, and the'straw is a more plentiful crop than has ,been the case for some time. In 1911 there was a „ good season, but it is eclipsed by the present • crops, which go a long way to make amends fOr the bad times formerly experienced. The early potato crop has sold well, thanks to the dearth of old potatoes earlier in the spring. The mid. crop is proving profitable, and the late potatoes promise well. Turnips are growing nicely, only they require more moisture- than we have had up to the time of writihg, but, then, farmers cannot have everything they want.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
GRANT FOR CATTLE BREEDING.

At last we have something definite about the long-promised scheme for the improvement of cattle breeding, brought out by the debate in the House on the Agricultural Vote. Mr. Runci- man, in his reply to questions and criticisms, went over much ground, and gave a consider- able amount of information. Among this we find the announcement that approved societies, not trading for profit, will receive an annual grant of £l5 per bull, kept for the use of 'its members, and where no suitable society exists, or can be formed, an individual farmer who keeps a pedigree bull for the use of his neigh- bours will receive a grant of -£l2. It is desired to encourage the co-operative principle, and hence the larger sum to be given to societies. Evidently the scheme will require a good deal of working out in its details, otherwise there will be many failures. There are few districts where the cattle are so uniform in breed and character that one breed or strain in the bulls selected will suit all cows. For instance, some farmers will want to improve in the direction of beef, and others in that of milking capacity. Mr. Runciman himself did not express much enthusiasm, saying that he had not much hope of any startlingly good effect, but did hope there might be a gradual improvement.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
GRANTS FOR MILK RECORDS.

Another new departure was announced by the President of the Board, and one which may have Ifar-reaching effects. Farmers as a class are not considered very good bookkeepers, but In the last few-years there has been some pro- gress made-in this direction. In no branch of their business, however, have they so grievously neglected their accounts as in that of keeping a record of the milk yield of their cows. It is a practice which we have often urged in the Press, having found its great value by personal experience. The Dairy Shorthorn movement has forced the subject to the front, and now we have the beginnings of a reform through the agency of the Development Fund. The tone of Mr. Flunciman's remarks on the new proposals was far more sanguine than those in which he alluded to the cattle-breeding grants. Lie pro- phesied that in ten years' time it would be said that the best day's work ever done by the Board was when it started milk-recording societies. These bodies are to be voluntary and co-opera- tive, and are to receive a grant of £5O per an- num towards their expenses. They will employ a "tester," whose duty it will be to visit each member's premises and weigh the milk at regu- lar and stated times, and, it may be presumed, will also test its quality, which, in these days, Is scarcely less important than its qUantity. This scheme, if extensively adopted, Must have a good educational effect, and tend to open the eyes of dairy farmers to the necessity of know- ing which cows are paying their way and which are not.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

A FEW USEFUL HINTS.

Stake Chrysanthemums and tie as growth proceeds. Hoe the soil around the plants, and give a stimulant..

As. a stimulant for Sweet Peas old fowl manure is as good as anything. Use in a weak state, little and often. Take cuttings of perennial Phl*xes. and plant In boxes of sandy soil, stand in a cold frame 'shaded from the sun. •

Overgrown Peonies may be pulled into pieces of moderate size and replanted in soil well prepared and manured. Make the soil firm by treading. an give the plants a good watering. Mint is often attacked by rust, a fungus pro- ducing orange or orange-brown spots on the leaves, which, if not checked, renders the Mint useless. The only remedy is to pull up and burn all diseased stems.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME USEFUL PLANTS.

Some of the older flowering plants are now-a- days almost forgotten. - Galega, or Goat's Rue, is one that deserves cultivation, and will pro- vide an abundance of pea-shaped flowers for in- door decoration.

The common Evening Primrose (Enothera biennis) is a useful plant, the flowers closing during the day. A more attractive variety is (Enothera Fruiticosa Youngu, the columnar stems of which are for some weeks furnished with brilliant golden-yellow blossoms. The buds are enclosed in a sheath of orange-scarlet hue.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
GROWING OF BROCCOLI.

Broccoli intended for cutting early in the new year require • to be grown in a richer soil than those that mature later. The reason for this is that a rich soil means quicker growth and more tender and sappy plants, which would succumb under severe frost. Manure is not required for late crops, so as to make a more hardy growth. The best position to plant, is where the early Potatoes were grown. The soil will not re- quire 'digging, but loosen deeply with the hoe, and make the plants firm.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
HARVESTING POTATOES.

In digging potatoes, the weather and soil should be, if possible, both dry, as nothing is worse than having them wet at taking-up time, or indeed at any time after they are taken up. If dug when wet, the Soil adheres to them, and makes them both unsightly and not easily dried. After September potatoes should only be dug in the mornings on "fine. 'days, and if they are thrown well .on the surface of the Soil they will dry a good deal before 'night. esnecially- if the soil under them is .moderately dry, and they - should be placed under -cover before night. Greening is one of the worst faults an eating potato can have; and this must be avoided at harvesting time, and the sooner therefore they can be dried and the quicker stored the better. When digging up potatoes, the tubers for dif- ferent purposes should be selected. All those for eating should be secured first, as it is im- portant to get them out of the light, and it does not matter mucli if those for seed are left some days on the soil.

A NEW RAMBLER ROSE,

A welcome addition to the rambler class of Roses is Excelsa, wiiich, in the opinion of a writer in "The Garden," will before long oust Crimson Rambler from most gardens. The soft yet bright cherry colour of the flowers are far more pleasing than those of the old variety. Excelsa is a Wichurainana Rose, has a vigorous habit similar to that of Dorothy Perkins, and flowers freely.- There is another Rose in the Wichurainana class which is not grown'so--much as it ought to be. Sweetly scented, of a creamy-white-yellow coinur, the blooms are charming amongst the dark shiny, foliage. Alberic Bar Wan is a very vigorous grower, and reouitys little pruning.

The Lyon Bose has given us'sorne glorcous examples this season, the flowers in many gar- dens being of a richer eopnery stirimo-nink colour than rireviotis years. The Lyon Bose is ft CCM.

Early flowering elimbina varieties, sueh as Tea Rambler, Polyantha Simplex. and Carmine Pillar. may have the old flowering cut out, in order to encourage young growths. , ' . Dwarf Polvantha Roses form n ilrrl edqtriC to beds of Roses. being free-flowering, very dwarf in habit, and variety of colour.

No machine has yet been invented in France which, can supersede manual labour in the manu- facture of champagne bottles. The men per- forming this difficult work are well paid. .

/• ,A.n electric process for keeping the hulls of ships free from barnacles has been invented. Boats fitted with the opna,ratus. it is claimed, will never be hampered in speed by barnacles, and the life of the ship's hull will be longer.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
Such a Society Needed.

The opening ceremony should have been per- formed by Mrs. Broadbent, but illness militated against her being present, and the function was gracefully performed by her daughter, Miss Sylvia Broadbent. The Rev. G., C. BRIGGS (president) said...that was a moment of pride for Latchford—it was a memorable occasion. He thought the time had arrived for a garden society. They were courted by the late and lamented "Beautiful Warring- ton Society." Then a handsome, prosperous young man in the Stockton Heath Garden Soc- iety, paid them much attention. Whether things had gone so far that either of them might have brought an action for, breach of promise against them, he did not know; they risked it. 4 Laughter.) They preferred to remain single. They were an independent people at Latchford, .and preferred doing 'things on their own. There was certainly room for sucli a society. Latchford, with its numerous gardens and gar- den,..plots, was a suburb which lent itself admir- ably to the objects of a garden society. One object of such a society was to teach men to love and care for what was beautiful, and to make the district in which it operated a beautiful locality. It did that partly by means of healthy competition. It wanted men to vie with one another in producing what was beautiful, and it 'offered prizes for the best." Such an object was deserving of success. Would their society suc- ceed? He was hopeful it would, and if the people supported their energetic committee it would.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
Pleasurable. Pursuit.

. - Miss SYLVIA BROADBENT declared the show open in a neat little speech. She deeply regretted that her mother had been seized with indisposi- tion, as she had eagerly looked forward to at- tending their first show. "L am passionately fond of flowers," said Miss Broadbent, " and, I must confess, I am garden- ing when I should be doing something else." (Laughter and applause.) in regard to the Stockton Heath Society,. she thought they ought to be grateful to the latter for the helping hand they had given to their new society. She thought the first show of the Latchford Society was a success. (Hear, hear.) The exhibits were beau- tiful and did the greatest credit to the exhibitors. (Applause.) "My mother has asked me," said Miss Broad- bent, to read the message she had prepared. She says : " I am much pleased to have been asked to open the first flower show of the Latchford Soc- iety. There is hardly any pursuit that gives .more pleasure than the cultivation of flowers, fruit, and vegetables. I say nothing of the good that is gained by the time spent in the open air. I hear the display to-day is most creditable, and as each year comes round there will probably be an improvement in the exhibits. " ' The committee wisely decided to have tho show in this building, for in our uncertain climate one is not able to trust to a fine day long beforehand. But the weather is perfect to-day. and augurs well for the success of the show, and I hope that after seeing the exhibits the visitors will spend some time in our adjoining home-field. I am sure the committee have worked- hard to obtain such a good result, and I congratulate them heartily. I. have been to see some Of the gardens -which have won prizes, and am much impressed by their gay anpearance. I hope to visit the others during the next few days.'" (Applause.)

NOT HIS HOME.

Warrington Man's Strange Mistake.

A strange mistake which followed as a sequel to a woms picnic cin Saturday, was, mentioned at Widnes Police Court on Monday, when Joseph Itimmer, of Esther-street, Warrington, was charged with entering the dwelling-house of G. Law, Crouton-lane, and doing damage to the extent of 7s. 6d. Supt. Foster said that Rimmer was a fore- man at a Warrington works. On Saturday he had been to a picnic. Between one and two o'clock on Sunday morning Law beard a noise in the•kitchen, and on going downstairs he saw 'the prisoner, who waved his clenched fist at him. Law hurried back to the bedroom, and, getting through the window, dropped to the ground, with the object of getting assistance. He roused several neighbours, and they sur- rounded the house, while others sought the vil- lage constable. The prisoner, however, escaped into the gar- den at the back 'of the house and bid himself in the buslies. When one of the-search party dis- covered him, he shouted, "Hands up, or I will shoot." Ile was at length secured and handed over to .the.police. The prisoner's explanation was that he had been to the works picnic. The party were re- turning through Gateacre late at night when one of the conveyances broke down near Cron- ton. He was very much the worse for drink and wandered across the adjoining field. On reaching the cottage next to -Law's he evi- dently thought he was at home. for it was found that he had pushed the key through a bole in the door, and afterwards smashed Law's win- dow and climbed into the kitchen. The Bench dismissed the case, and the pris- oner left the dock declaring that he would never touch drink for the remainder of his life.

A bell in a temple in North China has been kept ringing for a century. A tax is levied in the district for paying relays of ringers to work incessantly day and night.

BURDEN. OF BELFAST FtcpUTS.

A LESSON--TIiAT MAY DO SOM g- GOOD,

Underwriters at Lloyds have announced their intention of increasing their, premiums from Monday next for insuring against the risk of damage to property in Belfast next year. They will charge 10 per cent. after this week. We cannot pretend to be sorry that this should be so. It will be a good thing if the people of Belfast are brought, to realise that their present policy may be an expensive business for them- selves. A feature of nearly all the big riots in the past in Belfast has been the looting of the shops by the ruffians of both parties, and the time is coining when the more respectable citi- zens are asking themselves why .they should bear .the burden of all the faction fights. The kind of talk there has been in the North of Ire- land of late is taken as a licence to loot and damage prOperly by every ruffianly, element in tlie population. Belfast is becoming too' wealthy and notable a 'city to regard the opera- tions of these gentry with calmness, and the shopkeepers and manufacturers who have. to bear, the cost of the incitements to disturbance are likely to make their influence felt in local' opinion as the fact dawns upon them that they are the destined victims of any trouble which may be created.—" Westminster Gazette."

The wife of Herr Ottman, a well-to-do manu- facturer at Schonthal. Bavaria, has given birth to seven children within fourteen months. In. April, 1912, four girls were born to her at the same time, and a week or two ago she gave birth to triplets, all three boys. The most powerful locomotive in the world has just been built by the American Locomotive 'Company for the Virginian railroad. It can haul 155 loaded 'fifty-ton capacity goods trucks at ten miles an hour. It has sixteen driving wheels. The locomotive and tender weigh 752 00011).. and the firebox is large enough to hold a shunting locomotive.

PRETTY PENKETH FLOWERS.

Horticultural Exhibits a Marked Improvement on Last Year.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THR CAGE BIRD SHOW.

"The show is a great improvement all round upon last year's. A special feature is the violas and roses. The violas would win in any show. and the first prize was 'well won,' it standing out well ahead of all others. There is good competition, and the vegetable section is up to standard."

Such was the flattering report of the judges, made at the opening of the 20th annual exhibi- tion of the Penketh, Sankey and District Garden and Cage Bird Society, held on Saturday in a field at Warrington-road, Penketh. The Chairman (Mr. William Woods) said he could corroborate all the judges had said. • A very high state of perfection, had been reached, and while they had catered for adults, they had also given encouragement to the children, and they had entered enthusiastically into the corn- pe_tition.•

"It is," he said, "a splendid idea to train the children in the cultivation of flowers, as it engrains in them a love of Nature and a love of the beautiful. In years to come the show will not only reap benefit from the encouraging of the childien, but the children are reaping bene- fit through being interested in their young days in the culture of flowers. We should do every- thing to encourage them." (Applause.) Mrs. Dodds, declaring the show open, said next year the society would come of 'age. She thought it had done, and would continue to do, an excel- lent work in encouraging the residents to culti- vate pretty gardens.

WARRINGTON AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY SHOW

Excellent Display of Horses and Cattle. Fine Jumping by Crack Hunters. ONE OF 111 E, MOST SUCCESSFUL EXHIBITIONS HELD.

A Very Compact, little show, classes neatly Arranged, and every animal shown possess- int) outstanding points of merit. The Shire , horses an excellent collection, the animals being well built and in the best possible con- he hackneys and hunters constituted fine classes. The jumpers included some of the most famous in the country. Dairy cows sPlendid; pigs fair. On the whole, a first- class show.

call"' these words one of the judges, who had y:ettillY inspected all the stock in the show- atef the Warrington Agricultural Society r of Hall, on Thursday, expressed the t,e,era l opinion of the public. bjlts, the 24th, show was certainly one of the 1,,",,‘• There was a large entry of animals, th'u the pick of the district was represented in lings and pens. , The number of entries ,277, an increase upon that of last year, Viet' show .suffered through the foot-and- :tall disease outbreak last season. exhibits was that at the 'stand of Alessrs. Gartons, Limited, the well-known Warrington firm of seed experts, who displayed some strik- ing sheaves of their new and regenerated breeds of seed wheats, which are now so largely cultivated in all parts of the country. The new breed of white wheat, " Victor," which is so largely cultivated in this district, looked. re- markably well, as also did the new regenerated breeds of "Squareheads Master" and "Browick" wheats,. which have Proved to be the most pro- fitable red wheats in cultivation.

The new breed of oats, "The Leader," which has yielded 20 quarters per acre, also occupied a prominent position in the exhibit, and a care- ful examination of this showed it to be greatly superior to all other oats .hitherto introduced.

A miscellaneous collection of other agricul- tural plants was also displayed, including some mammoth specimens of a new breed of red clover, grown on the experimental farm at Acton Grange.

AN EXCITING MOMENT.

While the jumping was in progress a bride- less horse, with a:- trap attached, came racing down upon the crowd. For a --moment it seemed as if it would plough into the spectators. It had carried away the award board, but a number of police succeeded _ in diverting its course before. it reached the ring, and, after a struggle with the excited animal, it was brought to a halt.

The judging finished rather late, and unfor- tunately the weather broke towards the end of the show, and rain fell heavily. The band of the Cheshire Yeomanry provided a well-assorted programme of music.

Photograph by Mr. Burrell.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 23, 1913. IN THE COURTS.

A 1'1101.% LING THIEF.

had threatened him. He never abused either of the men previously—he never hit Alannion with a poker, nor did he burst Mitchell's nose. Witness had been to Warrington with produce, but .was not so drunk that he fell off the hurry. He had some beer, but there was no complaint against him by the master. He had been in the habit of buying food for these men, but had never bought potatoes for them. It did not come to his knowledge that these men had been saying he had been stealing potatoes and brass and lead during carting work, from the Asylum. He did bring a bag out of the Asylum, but he did not know what was in it. He had not " cleared " before the prisoners came home. The defence was that at the most only .a com- mon assault was committed in the shant, and that the prosecutor came by his injuries out- side by other -means.

Pris'oners were committed for trial at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

Scheme That Causes the Police Much Trouble.

FITTING SENTENCE.

Elizabeth Lavin, 24, Portland-street, wife of Thomas Lavin, was charged on remand, at the Warrington Borough Police Court on Thursday, with stealing from the dwelling-house, 19, Port- land-street, an alarm clock, value is. 11d., the property of Patrick Murray, and also with steal- ing from the dwelling-house, 8, Litton's-row, a white cotton quilt, a flannelette nightdress, and a shawl, value Os. 10d., the property of Julia Highani. The case having been proved, Superintendent Williamson said there were many previous eases against the prisoner. in 1911 she was bound over on a charge of stealing whisky. For stealing boots and a sweeping brush she had been sent to. gaol for three months; stealing hop ale, three months; and articles from a shop, three months. .Last April she was sent for three months for stealing a dolly peg. She made a practice of going round the town to houses and stealing wherever possible. She did not go to a pawnbroker's shop, or second- hand dealer, but went to another part of the town to sell the stolen property privately, wher- ever she could. This was a source of great trouble to the police, who had to make system- atic calls from house to house to make inquiries in order to trace the stolen goods. Prisoner was sent to gaol for six months-- three months in each case.

IS IT RIGHT?

Beer in Transit in Open Cases.

QUESTIONS BY THE BENCH.

At the Warrington Borougn rolice Court on Thursday -two young married men, Alfred Wood- line, 6, uldham-street, number taker, and John Hardman, 22, Lloyd-street, goods porter, were charged in custody with having, on August 20th, stolen from a case in the Vareliouse of the London and North-Vv estern .Railway Company at Bank Quvy, a bottle of beer, value 4d., the property of the company. . Sergeant Howard, a police officer in the em- ploy of the London and North-Western Railway Company, stated that at a quarter to eleven on the previous night he was "watching " at Bank Quay, in company with Detective Wisley. There were some cases of bottled beer standing on the stage. Woodfine took up a bottle, had a drink, and handed it on to Hardinan. A clerk then came out of the office and disturbed them; and they tried to hide themselves behind a crate. Woodfine said he found the bottle, and Hard- man said the bottle was handed to him by Woodfine to have a drink.

The Magistrates' .Clerk (Mr. White) : Does the company suffer from this sort of thing? Witness:• Yes; complaints are very frequent. Superintendent Williamson: And the Railway Company wish it to be dealt with, bearing that fact in mind? Witness: Yes. The Chairman: Had they to break the case open? Witness: No; they are open cases in transit, with two dozen bottles each. The detective corroborated the sergeant's evi- dence. In cross-examination he denied that prisoners had found the bottle. . Mr. Upson (from the Bench) : It was possible for anyone to take this bottle from the case? Witness; Yes. • •

Is it a wonder you have heavy losses if you allow that?—Some are closed, but these were not. . .

Superintendent Williamson: They were just open boxes?—Yes; they were from Walker's Brewery.

Mr. Upson: These boxes have to be handled by a considerable number of men?—Yes; they were on the stand and left there. <. - Woodfine said he did not take the bottle out of the case, so the officers Could not have seen what they said they -saw. The Chairman said they were sorry to see two young 'men in the dock on a charge of this kind. They had no right.-to, take this beer in any case, but the Bench would he lenient, on the distinct understanding that they -never did this kind of thing again, . .

Both pro-m- ised -tha-t they would not, and they were each fined 10s., including costs, or seven days.

STOLE FOR WEDDING GIFT.

Sad Story of a Young Girl Out of

Control.

WORSE SINCE t GOING TO• LYMM.

A sad story of a ,Young girl's unpromising start in life was told at the Warrington Police Court on Thursday morning. The magistrates on the Bench were Messrs. W. Roberts (in the chair), Dr. Naden, Alderman James Evans, and Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Upson and W. T. Williams.

Nellie Lowe (16), 49, West-street, step-daugh- ter of Robert Donaldson and domestic servant, was charged by Adele Willis, 31, Legh-street, with stealing six teaspoons, value 4s. Superintendent Williamson stated that the prisoner had been servant for Mrs. Willis for three weeks, and there had been some trouble with her. The girl obtained permission to go to a friend's wedding, and later the girl's mother brought these spoons to Mrs. Willis, ind asked her whether they were hers. Mrs. Willis found that the spoons were hers, and had bcen taken from the house and given to tilt girl's friend as a. wedding gift. This friend had brought the spoons to the, girl's mother. Pri- soner admitted having taken the spoons, as she wanted to give them as a present. She was completely out of the control of her mother and had only recently stayed out all night with another girl and two young men. She had been in previous situations and had admitted she had been in the habit of stealing small items of change from. her mistresses. Prisoner said if she was given a chance she would be a good girl and do as her mother told. The mother entered the witness box and said she had done all she could for her daughter, who persisted in staying out late at night. They had locked her out, thrashed her, and tried kindness, but it was all of no avail. She had been worse during the last twelve months—since she went-to the .Lymm cutting -shop. She went to Lymin against. their wishes and lived, in lodgings. The Chairman said it was painful to hear mother say she had no control over it sixteen-. year-old daughter.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
The Last Chance.

The Magistrates' Clerk (tti the girl) : Are rou willing to go to a home? Prisoner (sobbing) : Oh, no, sir! Let me go home. I will be a good girl if you will try me this once. The Chairman (having consulted with the rest of the Bench) : We are going to give you a chance, but it is the last chance you will have. We are going to put you under the control and observation of the Probation Officer for twelve months. That means that you will be bound over to behave yourself. The officer will have his eye on you and report to us from time to time. If you take my advice you will give up these companions, and when you have your nights off spend them at home. Otherwise you may land yourself into very serious trouble. If you come here again you are bound to be sent to prison. Now, go, and let this be a warning .to ,you. Prisoner, who had agreed with every word of advice as the Chairman uttered it, then left the dock.

IRISH SHANT SCENE.

Alleged Assault With a Poker at Winwick. _

_ At an Occasional Court, held on Friday .at, Warrington, •before Mr. George .Mather, Joseph Mitchell and Thomas .Mannion were charged on remand with unlawfully wounding 'l.`nemas Healey, farm labourer, Beech House, Winwick, on August 15th. ' Mr. Knowles appeared for the defence. Prosecutor stated that he was in bed in the farm shant shortly after ten o'clock on the date in question. The prisoners came in and Mitchell got on to the bed and kicked him. Witness told him to get off, and he came again with a poker with which he struck witness on the back four times. Mannion kicked witness out of the shant, followed him into the road, and struck him on the head three times with the poker. Both sides of the head were cut and six stitches had been put in by the doctor. Witness went off to hide, but Mannion followed and assaulted him near the Smithy, but he eventually-got away, and made his way towards Warrington. He was overtaken by two of Nayloes men. who took him to the police station. He was then taken to the Infirmary, where he had been detained until that (Friday) morning. When be was being hit he shouted for mercy, but Mitchell said. "I will kill you; the policeman has gone on duty."

MONDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Bol- ton, Smethurst and James Evans, and Messrs. Wilkinson, Roberts, Pilling and Furness. . ... _

A TAXI TO THE LOCE-Up.—Samuel Worrell, no fixed abode, had a taxi ride to the police cells on Saturday night. He was now charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Knutsford- road, and with assaulting the police.—P.C. Good- man said Worrall was so violent-that he had to charter a taxicab to get him to the Police Sta- tion. The assault consisted of kicking the officer. Worrall, who said he could remember nothing about the affray, was fined ss. and costs. THEY WOULD FIGHT.—The police intervened in a fight between James Leonardy, no fixed abode, Warrington, and Thomas Price, who hails from Ellesmere Port, and they were charged with that offence. At the Police Station, said Superinten- dent Williamson, Price behaved like a madman. although he was perfectly sober. They would persist in fighting when the officer asked them to stop.—Leonardy, who had -past records in the police books, was fined 10s. and costs, with, the option of 14 days, and Price had to make choice between a fine of ss. and costs or seven days. KICKED THE CONSTABLE.—Thomas Collins, no fixed abode, was discovered drunk in Pinner's Brow on Saturday, and told the police that he would not be taken to the lock-up. He then kicked P.C. Norris in the abdomen and on the legs, and generally became very violent. He was now charged with assaulting the police and being drunk, of which charges he said he was guilty.—Superintendent Williamson said Collins was now 22 years of age, and had started to get into trouble with the police when a boy of 12. He must be warned that he could not assault the police as he liked.—The Mayor said Collins had committed e dangerous assault, but they would give him a chance once-more.—A fine of 10s., with the option of 14 days in prison, was imposed.

CRANE HANDLE BROKE.

Tdrrible Accident at the Dalton Bank Works.

A LABOURER'S DEATH.

The sad fatality which occurred at the Dalton Bank Works of, Messrs. Rylands on Friday of last week, was investigated by Coroner Brig- house at an inquest held. on Saturday. The victim was Martin Lane, 49 years, a labourer, of 22, Gandy-street, Warrington. The Coroner explained that five Men were cm- gaged turning the handle of a large crane used to open an iron door, which weighed 20 cwt., attached to a furnace. The door was Ibuu: three-quarters open, when the handle broke, and caused four of the men to fall. The brokan part, which remained on the crane, revolved rapidly and struck Lane on the head, killing him. The man had been employed at the works 23 years. Joseph Gallop, labourer, 10, Anne-street, said they were engaged hoisting the number six door of the gas furnace when the accident happened. It took five or six men to hoist the door, as it weighed about a ton. They had used the same crane handle that day on two previous (Mors. When hoisting the sixth door the crane handle broke at the elbow. Three or four men fell to the floor, and the part of the handle on the crane whirled round and struck Lane in the .face. The first blow knocked him unconscious, and the broken handle, coming round at a great speed, Struck him seVeral times in the chest, tearing the flesh open. The handle had been about a year in use, .and the doors had been opened with it every day. The Coroner: No one had any idea that the handle was defective?—No; the foreman exam- ined it before we started Mr. Brothers (factory inspector): The metal is crystalise.d, and ,crystalisation takes place from continuous use.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Could Not Have Been Foreseen.

The Coroner said that could not have been foreseen. If it could have been the employers would be to blame, but for what they could not see or anticipate they could ngt be held to blame. They could not look inside the iron to see what changes were 'taking place, and could only make an external inspection to see if there were defects.

Mr. Brothers (to witness): Did the furnace door stick?—No, sir.

What was the position of the handle when it broke?—lt was half-way up. .10n Sutton, foreman at the works, said that the handle had been in use about twelve months.

The Coroner: From the length of time the handle had been in use, could you form any opinion as to whether its condition was good or bad? I thought it was safe enough for any- thing. You cannot tell us the reason of the fracture? —No; the handles are mad d by our own black- smith, and they are supposed to be of the best irOn and workmanship. Mr. Brothers: The crystalisation was working in the handle. Could you see it?—No; there was no -outward •evidence. The iron is of the best. quality.

No Defects Visible.

you.cannot.tell if There was any defect in the quality of the iron?—No.

Is there any reason why this door should be worked by man power?—l could not say. The overhead crand is worked by a motor?— Yes; an electric motor.

Could you not raise the 'door by mechanical means?—l don't know.

J. W. Jeffrey, foreman mechanic, said his Opinion was that .the breaking of the crane handle was due to the continual working of the handle, which had caused crystalisation in the iron, and that would considerably weaken it. The Coroner: Was it latent or patent?— Latent.

Could you tell its presence by seeing the handle?—No, it is beyond the power of man to see inside a bar of iron. I had put a glass on it, and I could not see anything wrong. The Coroner: The same thing happens in the case of draw bars and chain links used in pits. Five out—of 'six might stand the tests and the sixth give way?—Yes, sir. '

Cannot Protect Themselves.

This is one of the defects against which you

cannot protect yourselves?—Yes. I should think this handle has been in use night and day for about 15 or 18 months.

It has been suggested that the opening of the furnace doors could be done otherwise than by manual labour. What do you say?—My idea Is that as mechanic it can be done by mech- anical means, and we shall be pleased to give consideration to any suggestions you, sir, or Mr. Brothers may make. It would, however, do away with labour?— Yes.

Then there would be a row, added the Coroner to the jury. You would be out of the frying pan into the fire, and you would have some Vade unionists complaining about having mech- anical power instead of human labour. (Laugh- ter.) Mr. Brothers: You could not detect anything wrong by inspection of the handle?—No. You might have a handle, which, quite un- known to you, is weak?—lt is possible.

Mechanical Means.

I submit you have a danger here which ought to be obviated entirely by the adoption of mech- anical means. How often are these handles tested?—Every twelve months. Mr. Brothers: An inspection every - twelve months is not sufficient.

Mr. A. E. Connor (for the relatives): Assuming you have suspected crystalisation, could you detect it by any means?—No. The only way you could find out was to open the bar?—Yes.

Were these handles made to bear a ton strain, or was there a margin?—There was a margin. - What ,were they made to stand?—Double the weight required to he lifted. They have a'power strain of I,ooolb. per man or so?—We make them strong enough to lift four or five times the weight. - The work this handle.had to do was to lift a ton?—Yes, and it was made strong enough to lift four .or. five tons. There may have been something Particular happen at that door. The other five. were lifted .easily. I would not like to commit myself as to what took place. Five Deaths in 35 Years.

Witness, in answer to Mr: Jenkins, for the firm, said he had been employed 35 years with Messrs. Rylands, and they had had no serious accident in the working of the doors previously. He could not remember a serious accident there in the 35 years: This was about the fin fatal accident at the works in his experience, an-I, unfortunately, two of the five had happ.tneti that week. They employed 2,000 men. Mr. Jenkins: Is the broken handle of good quality?—Yes,; it was,made by one of our cwn blacksmiths with the greatest care, and is intide of the very best iron. After a handle is made the foreman blacksmith careluily inspects it.

The Coroner: During the time y.. 0 have had these 'handles in use, you have had factory Inspectors through the works without. any sug- gestion being made that this method was -a wrong method?—l have never heard one say anything. • . .

bo.yo-u know of any statutory obligation or any rule or regulation you have broken?—No, sir.

The Coroner said Messrs. lands were a firm who had reputation, money and skill which would prevent them adopting inferior methods when they could make use of the best of methods. The method of lifting the furnace doors had now been in eistence.for five years, and neither the employes nor the inspectors, who visited the works, had said anything against it. Were they, as a jury, to step in now and say that it was wrong? The employes were now combined in a trade union, and against that he had nothing to say, and, if there was any defect apparent to them, they were strong enough, both physically and morally, to make 'a representation to the em- ployers about the matter.. It might be that some suggestions might be made both by the employes and the employers to obviate such an accident in the future, but he was not going to solve that problem. It was too great for hiin. (Laughter.) If there was any question, it ought to be left to the firm and the factOrY inspector. The jury found that death was accidental, and the Foreman said the jury hoped the factory inspector and the firm might be able to ,devise a. method to prevent such a mishap in the fliture. The jury expressed sympathy with the relatives, and Mr. Jenkins voiced the sym- pathy of the firm on the sad accident.

CHESTERFIELD.

The older generation of politicians wiTh remember the battle-cry of the Grand Old Man:l "The flowing tide is with us:" That cry was used in the promising days that followed upon the disaster of 1886, when his Home Rule pro- posals, then little understood and consequently feared, had caused a serious breach in the ranks of Liberalism. When the tide began to flow again the great leader looked back to 1885, and the "As in 1885" test was a sufficient guide to assure him of a return to power with a pros- pect of doing that act of justice to Ireland to which he dedicated the remaining portion of his days. We all know what happened in 1906. The Tories were almost wiped out of existence; if we can do again "As in 1906" we shall be perfectly satisfied at the next election. Chester- field has done so much better than in that year of horrors—from a Conservative point of view— that we shall be excused for feeling certain that the workers are beginning to see through the insincerities of the abuse of the Insurance Act, and to assess at its true value the kind of thing the Opposition are trying to pass upon the country for statesmanship. That being so,. there will be an increased probability of the return of the Government to power at the next erection to complete the social Programme upon which they and the workers of . the country have set their .hearts. The majority ,on Wed- nesday was 2,186, notwithstanding a third- candidate complication ; in 1906, hi a straigtit fight, it Was only 1,664. The result is a message to all and sundry that the Progressive 'forces are uniting behind the Government, and that the workers, of whom the Derbyshire miner is line type, will, when the time comes, confirm the Asourni Administration in office and give a renewed mandate to the greatest Government, judged by achieved results, of modern times. In this election the Progressive forces were .to some extent divided by Mr. RAMSAY Mk- DONALD'S repudiation of the miners' selection of candidate who had strong claims upon the combined support of Liberalism and Labour, and by the appearance of a Socialist candidate. Mr. KENYON'S Tory opponent, in his manifesto Upon the eve of the poll, played a card which has now little chance of winning an electoral trick, when he denounced the Insurance Act. The Tory candidate's appeal, apart'. frdm this, consisted in an assertion that the Liberal Gov- ernment had devoted ilself to questions like Home Rule, Disestablishment, and Plural Vot- ing, to the neglect of measures which would ameliorate the lot of the working classes. And Mr. CHRISTIE. went on to cpmplain that the Liberal Government had put forward no pro- posal which would raise the level of wages. If Tory electioneeters imagine that industrial con- stituencies are lukewarm upon the subject of the plural voting anomaly, they imagine a very vain thing. It would have been interesting to learn what proposals the Tory candidate at Chesterfield wished the electors to believe that his party were promulgating in order to raise the level of wages. Any Tory candidate who cari persuade himself that a working-class con- stituency would be attracted by a nebulous Tariff Reform • policy rsWhich the Tory leaders themselves /shrink from defining), and could possibly be induced to believe that such a policy could raise wages, can, only be appropriately described in American slang as an out-and-out back number.

The Home of the Swallow.

The teacher in natural history had received more or less satisfactory replies to her questions, and finally she ltsked : "What little boy can tell me where the home of the swallow is?" Long silence, then a hand waved. "Well, Bobbie, where is it?" "The home of the swallow," declared Bobbie, seriously, "is in the stum- mick."

Something Went Wrong.

Something went wrong with the works when Ling Soo was performing his magic bottle trick on Saturday afternoon. The bottle stuck half-way and the neck shot up with startling suddenness after the cover had been removed, and Ling Soo and his chief assistants had to complete the trick by brute strength.

Colonel Sykes' Anxiety. There was no lierson more anxious at the Warrington Show on Thursday than. Colonel Sykes, M.P. .During the day he haunted the Press tent with the one query :- "Have you heard the Chesterfield election result?" At a later hour the Press were able to afford the information that the Liberal was ,in. Then, disappointed, the Knutsford M.P. concentrated his thoughts upon the horse jumping. Doubt- less it was more interesting than ,the Tory defeat at Chesterfield.

Coincidence.

The fad that a Warrington man, who was alleged to have been stealing some workmen's suppers, was captured by P.C. Grubb recalls another similar coincidence. A pickpocket, who ran away, when captured and charged at Manchester gave the name of A. Runner, and a constable named B. Swift told how he caught the Runner.

Mark Scored Over the Lawyer. Mark Twain could be cutting on occasion. At a dinner party he had once made a simple yet very amusing speech., When he sat down, a lawyer rose, and, putting, his hands in his pockets, remarked, patronisingly: "Rather Un- usual this, gentlemen, don't_ you think, for a

professional humorist to be funny?" When the laughter ceased: Twain drawled out: "Bather unusual, gentlemen, don't you think, for a lawyer to have his hands in his own pockets?"

And the Lawyer Annihilated, the Doctor.

Bobert Smith, brother of Stanley smut', and an ex-advocate general, on one occasion engaged in an argument with a physician. over the relative merits of their respective profes- sions.

"I don't say that all lawyers are crooks," said the doctor, "but you'll have to admit that your 'profession doesn't make angels cif men." "No," retorted Smith; "you doctors certainly have the best of us there."

Prepare tor Next Year. The early fly's the One to swat, It comes before the weather's hot, And sits around and.flies its legs, And lays at least ten million eggs. And every egg will bring a fly To drive us crazy by and by.

Yes, every fly ,that skips our swatters 'Will have ten million sons and daughters, And countless first and second cousins, And aunts and uncles, scores of dozens, And fifty-seven billion nieces,

So, knock the blamed thing all to pieces

And every niece and every aunt. Unless we swat them so they can't, Will lay enough confounded eggs To fill up ten five-gallon kegs; And all these eggs. ere summer hies. Will bring forth twenty million flies!

And thus it goes, an endless chain, So all our swatting is in vain, Unless we do that swatting soon— Say some time in the month of June. So. men and brothers, let us rise. Gird up our loins and swat the flies.

THE PASSING WEEK.

The show opened in brilliant weather on Thursday morning, but by noon heavy drops of rain fell upon Warrington like the first of a thunderstorm. The storm came not, but steady rain set in during the afternoon, and lasted intermittently through the night and yesterday. It was decidedly hard Upon the show . authorities, who thought the Clerk of the Weather was to be altogether pro- pitious, to have the attendance checked; but .the rain was very welcome notwithstanding, and the good it will do will more than wipe out the effects of its temporary inconvenience.

CRUELTY TO "POLITICIANS."

There ought to be a society for the prevention of cruelty to politicians. Even those rabid politicians who ktre so anxious for the downfall .of this wicked administration as •to believe it possible that Chesterfield could go wrong are worthy of our most anxious thought. On Wednesday the news "got out" that the Con- servative was "in," and the joy that rang oyer the wires between Sankey-street and certain Protectionist works knew' no bounds. The vibration set up was intense, and it is stated that yesterday the telephone repairing staff were particularly busy. It was all very ter- rible,' and what those who had been so cruelly wronged had to say on the subject has not been placed on record. Perhaps it is as well, for their reputation and the purity of their native tongue. A female prisoner in the Warrington dock on Thursday, when charged with being drunk, said she was "druv to it." A constable had warned someone with whom she was speaking as to her character: the truth "druv" her to drink. What the truth "druv" these men who rejoiced prematurely to do it would really be interesting to know.

THE LATE MR. HENRY HALLIRD.

The news of the death of Mr. Henry Hallard \vill be received with deep regret by many Warringtonians. Mr. llallard had travelled to Grange-oVer-Sands for a holiday, and died suddenly whilst there. Until two years ago, when he entered the service of, the County Council at Preston, he was a familiar figure in Warrington. In his capacity as Clerk of Works, he was engaged during a period of about a dozen years upon a number of build- ings in the town and neighbourhood, including the new Police Station, the Bolton and Beamont Council Schools, and several large shops in Bridge-street. • He combined a wide and accurate knowledge of building with a breezy and marked . personality. Those who knew him in business circles luiew him as both highly capable and original. Among his friends he was a welcome guest, a collector and retailer of admirable stories, and when he exercised his gifts as a comedian upon the plat- form he coutd hold and delight large audiences. A genial neighbour, he won a large circle of _

friends: to whom the news of his death will bring a keen Sense of. personal toss.

THE EISTEDDFOD IN AMERICA.

Those who read the article by " A.M." upon the historical side of the Welsh National Festi- val in to-day's " Examiner " may be interested to know that an Eisteddfod has been established upon a large scale in America.. The gathering has just been held at Pittsburg, and is thus described in the local newspaper: Twelve large stones erected in a circle, with one laid flat in the centre were arranged thus fpr the first time in America. Druids, bards and ovites, in the gowns of white, blue and green, respectively entered the sacred circle, and the ceremonies began. The unititiated at the Gorsedd gath- ered around the outside of the circle in hun- dreds, but could not pass the stones, each of. which was guarded by a bard. As Dyfed, the arch-druid, or as he is known on the hotel register, the Rev. Evan Rees, mounted the flat logan stone in the centre of the circle, a trumpet announced the assembling of the bards, while Dyfed called forth: "Corn Gwlad, is the country here?" Three times he asks: "Is there peace?" and three times the bards answer: " Heddwch," which echoes in Welsh the last word of Dyfed's question. The robed members then touched the sheathed sword held by Dyfed. which signifies that peace is present. Dyfed then proclaimed In Welsh that the Eisteddfod was open. Prof. William Apmadoc, of Chicago, sang, to the accompaniment of the Welsh harp, and a long progranime of speeches and music followed. In the afternoon the great song-feast opened in earnest.

THEATRE MANAGER'S NEW APPOINT-

MENT.

Mr. Manville Morton, the resident manager, is severing his connection with the Theatre Royal this week to take up the management of the Palace, West Bromwich. Mr. Morton took over the management of the Royal six months ago under very trying circumstances, but since then has proved himself an able producer with the Royal Stock Company, whose productions showed the stamp of his master hand. He has made many changes for the betterment of the theatre, and placed it on a higher standard than it has attained for many years, and his en- deavours for his patrons here sluvuld carry with him their wishes for his success in his new sphere.

THE MAN AND HIS WORK.INEWSPAPER OFFICE DAY.

posed for the position of chairman, but he earned, the respect and life-long friendship of Mr. Roberts by giving way for him, and con- senting to act as his lieutenant. Now Alder- man Burton has succeeded to the place of his former chief, and every member who.. serves under hini on the 'committee knows his worth, values his opinion, and would follow his lead loyally and unhesitatingly. The history of the committee, in which Alder- man Button looms sOlargelY, is full of interest, though it is one of figures and money to a large. extent. The first record of a gift to the funds of the Infirmary from the working classes dates back to 1867, when the mill meii of Dallam Iron Works contributed 12s. 6d. Two years later, in 1869, the workmen of the Warrington Wire and Iron 'Company, Ltd., gave £3 10s. .6d., and in 1872 we find the item " From different works in toWn, .Z 42. In 1874, the first 'Hospital Saturday collection realised ,E 45 4s. Id., and the collections prospered, ,so that in the ten years ended 1879 there, had been collected a total of £734 is.

The Work of the Committee. It was in 1880 that the committee was pro- perly organised, and that year produced £165 19s. 4d. In the thirty-two years that have fol- lowed, that sum has been made into a total of £22,497 155., a figure that shows at a glance how well the working classes have supported the institution, and how badly off the institu- tion would have been without their help. It isl the organisation, of the work .of the committee , that has made such a result possible. Alder- man Burton was the advocate of the systematic collection in works, by which it was possible to reach the total of £1,397 9s. 10d. in 1911. Unfortunately this amount was not equalled in 1912, when there Was a drop to £1,248. There were special reasons for this, arising out of the misunderstandings that have been caused in Connection with • the Insurance Act., We have no desire to introduce controversial 1 politics in an article of this description;. but we may be allowed to say that the near ap- proach of the collections last year to the record amount of the year before, shows that, the working men were not, in the main, inclined to be deterred , from doing ,their duty. We confidently expect that the present, year, when the misunderstandings have been cleared up, will witness a return to the upward move- ment that has almost continuously character- ised the results of the year's efforts. • A Successful Career.' • -

Alderman Burton., who rcsides at The Hollies, Liverpool-road, was born June 8, 1839, at Frads- Well, Staffordshire.' His father was a farmer, as his grandfather and great-grandfather had been. Early in life the hero of our sketch lost his father, which gave him the chance of show- ing the grit and self-reliance he had at his command. Successively he was employed at the famous ironworks of Messrs., Thornecrofts and Co., Wolverhampton,at Dalian' and Bew- sey Forges (to Which he .came in 1865), at the Whitecross Cp.'s works, and finally at Monks, Hall and Co.'s works,Nyhere he rose to be works manager. After having served the last-men- tioned firm for many years, loyally and well, and having earned and retained the goodwill and almost the affection of the men over whom he had been called to act, Alderman Burton retired in 1910,, amid a chorus of well wishes, and the fervently expressed hope that he would live long- in the happiest of retirements. The Infirmary has not absorbed all the service Alderman Burton has been willing to give to the .public. Ne entered .the Council so far back as. 1896, as ,a, representative of St: Austin's Ward, and his constituents were so satisfied with him that the, first was the only fight he Was ever called upon to make for his seat. In 1908 he was Made an alderman, in the place of the late Alderfruin James Fairclough, and in that position he is likely to remain as long as it pleases him to do so.

Alderman Burton, as such a man deserves to be, is happy at home. He was married on May 22 1864, and, all being well, he will complete his fifty years of married life next May. Of a family of twelve, eight survive, including four sons and four daughters.

The con- vict, pursued by warders, escapes over a roof 801 t.• from the ground, to whicri he climbs by means of a rope,' He gets upon an express train, and there are stirring -strugplas on the front, of tha engine and upon the roofs of the carriages, from which he is thrown. and run over by another train of detectives following in pursuit. He has a miraculous escape and finally his innocence is proved. There will be other powerfully. attraCtive films sfiown.

Some Impressions by a Visitor who saw the " Examiner " Enlarged. "PRIVILEGED HISTORIAN'S STORY." "We are,enlarging the paper by a column a page next Friday, so come as early as you like. We shall have to turn you loose by yourself, because we shall have our work cut out—but you know your way about." This was the Editor's " Good-bye " to me, as the luncheon hour, approached, on Tuesday, July 29th, the day on which the new and larger " Examiner " had had a preliminary run, in view of the week-end production. I had asked for, and been granted readily, facilities for see- ing " copy " go right through a printing office, from the Editor's hand to its appearance in the paper. The Composing Room. Early on Friday, August Ist, the Editor was in his sanctum when I was shown in. A moment afterwards I had been spirited away to the composing room. Right ahead were the new linotype machines, in full operation. The mechanic, no longer anxious, occasionally tapped a nut, or effected a trilling adjustment, while a boy, with more frequency, brought forward lumps of. type metal, all of a certain size, for the linotype machine is a heavy eater. Whereas the hand compositor sets one letter at once, the linotype Operator, with his marvellous and intricate machine, turns out a whole "line o' type," Which trickles down a chute and takes its place In the ever-growing column. The motto of the " lino " machine, like the famous advice on how to learn poetry, seems to be "Line upon line." . _

After having looked in at the reporters' room, I With a certain degree of awe, I resumed my I place in the composing department, where all I was activity and unmistakable geniality. Among other things, I saw a special news article set up from the manuscript. Incidentally I wondered how the authors of some of those manuscripts would feel if they could glance at their treasured pages when the latter are passed up from the Editor's desk. A few touches from the journalistic pen, and the most humble script, provided it possesses the ring of actuality, is transformed into readable English. But how chastened one feels when one's " copy " is scored and corrected by the coldest blue on earth—the editorial blue pencil As soon as the above-mentioned article was set up, a proof. was struck off; that is, the article was printed by itself on a long, narrow Strip of paper. This was taken to the "reader."• Not you, gentle reader! I mean the Press corrector, who goes by that name. His directions, not by any means confined to one proof, are written in the margin with a code of hieroglyphics, and from these the compositor revises the mass of type. All the type being "set up," it is assembled and made into full columns, and then into pages. If you kept your Souvenir Number, commemor- ating the Royal Visit, take it out and compare It with the current issue, both page by page, and column by column. This will, if you are of an observing turn of mind, convey to you some idea of the problems that fell upon every- body connected with enlarging the "Exam- iner."

New Conditions to Face. There were several new conditions to face, and the old, habitual tasks were to bef relegated to the realms of, the forgotten. It is only the young, or the vigorous, who can take up new habits. But no Warringtonian will accuse the " Examiner " of the crime of youthfulness. It must be, therefore, that the " Examiner " is vigorous. Downstairs, the new printing machine had begun its first week's task, and four of the staff, as well as the mechanic, kept keen eyes on the giant. When at rest he looked quite docile, but it was some time before this complication 'of cranks, wheels, tapes, rollers, laths, and what not, could be induced to pay proper respect to the paper specially provided for his consumption. When he is behaving himself as he should, his output is 3,000 sheets per hour, I was told. Sometimes he crumpled a corner; now and then he magnetised a sheet and clothed one of his ink rollers with it; again, just when he had printed an impression some impish temper caused him to rip the sheet ticross. But, then, any child can tear a piece of paper I These things were nothing to what he might , have done. Little by little, however, he shook himself into place. Here a tap, and there a turn of a screw, and as the day wore on—well, you know the rest—the enlarged paper duly came out. A Paper of Which to be Proud. In the course of a week many papers come Into your hands. Look at them closely; com- pare them with the " Examiner " ; criticise the type; examine the advertisements and the general get up. There is only one sentiment you can possibly entertain, if you carry out these important tests impartially. It is that of pride in the paper representing Warrington. It will be long before the " Examiner " of to- day is equalled; and when that day dod come, the " Examiner " itself will be still farther ahead. A Few Light Moments. Press day is a bustling time. But pervading the bustle at the " Examiner " Office is a fund of geniality—even on Press day. And light moments were frequent. One of the compositors confided to me an. aversion to setting up cricket scores. Cricket is slow sometimes; Bowling mains, I learnt, were not so pleasurable by the time they reach a newspaper office. Short stories, which are an "Examiner "feature, make life as a com- positor comparatively enjoyable, while an inter- esting Court case carries its excitement even to these sedrite surroundings. I should mention that I got most of these impressions from the hand-compositors. , Whether the "machine comps" have time to be similarly affected, I did not gather. The old war between the man of letters and the man of business was revived for a moment, when an old friend told me what he'd do with advertisers, who, he thought, were very nearly unmanageable in their demands upon the columns. "Which advertisement has the best place?" I asked of the "maker-up," who was then making up Page 2. "Why, this," he replied, pointing to Doan's well-known picture. "It comes the first on the page." He continued: "But what I would do if I was an advertiser is to get the space just above the police news. Everybody reads that, you know, and what's above it can't be missed."

A Towel and a Legend. Have you ever made the acquaintance of the printing office towel? I did. It came about in this way. The young giant, refreshed with oil and not yet steady, had thoughtlessly swal- lowed a half-sheet. He was turned open and we discovered the paper soaked in ink, tightly clinging to the roller. Rashly, I lent a hand; my left one. After clearing all the shreds, I was much bedaubed, and went aside, and found a slab of soap, and, hanging from a towel rail, what, must, have been a towel.

There is a legend about such a towel. It had been used well and often.. It eschewed water and refused to be washed. It became thin, and very stiff. One day the printer's boy banged the door and the towel shivered. It fell down, and was broken to bits by the fall.

The " Examiner " did not tell me that story ; but I humbly offer it to that member of the printing office staff who did tell me one. "Ex- change is no robbery." It is no part of my province to foreshadow the expectations and plans of the "Examiner," or to speak of the excellence of its various fea- tures; or, again, the advantages it offers to those who have anything to sell, which they think is worth advertising. I •am simply a Privileged spectator, and an equally privileged historian, of one of the great days of "The Warrington Examiner." W. G.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Alderman Thomas Burton. CHAIRMAN OF THE WORKINGMEN'S INFIRMARY COMMITThE.

The open-air musical festival, which was held in the Park last Sunday, calls pointed attention. to the great and ceaseless labours that are necessary to keep our leading chari- table institution in a state of healthy solvency year after year. _ _

Roughly speaking, it requires £5,000 annually to keep the Infirmary doors open to the suf- ferers, whom it sets itself to succour; and, endowments being unusually meagre, consid- ering the age and size of the Infirmary, and the importance of the town, continuous effort, has to be made to keep the coffers- replenished. There are many means by which money is brought to the Treasury, but among these means those adopted by the Working Men's Committee are unique in character, as they are satisfactory in result. Briefly, the committee consists of about :200 delegates from the various works in town, the pick of our working men, chosen by their fellows in the many 'shops out 'of respect for their .sterling character and known determina- tion to do all they can in the cause of humanity, regardless of trouble, loss of time, and, in many cases, the expenditure of hard-earned money: • A Good Leader. - - - -

• Mb At the head of this fine band of workers stands Alderman Thomas Burton, the chair- man, since the death. of Councillor 'John Roberts in 1906. These twO ran in double harness for many years, the Tory being chair- man, and the Liberal deputy-chairman. In the long distant past. when it was proposed to organise this body, Mr. Burton, keen then as now, in supporting the Infirmary, was pro-

===== 0002601_19130823_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PICTUREDROME.

"Alone in the Jungle" is an exceptionally fine film on view at the Picturedrotne. After a stirring lion hunt, the young daughter of a settler gets lost in the forest. Fearing that she may become a prey to the wild- denizens of the woods, a band of searchers is hurriedly organ- ised, with the girl's lover as leader. She is tracked by a man-eating lion, and just as it is about to spring upon her, the rescue party arrives and the lion is shot dead. The other films are of equally as tlgh a merit.

Alleged Ingratitude.

Coroner Brighouse told a Warrington jury on Saturday that he had, through the aid of the police, uiscovered that the Parents of a boy who had been knocked down by a motor car and killed, had misused the A:2 he had given them from the poor box. Ile alleged that the money had been mis-spent, and. added: "That shows how careful one must be in dispensing charity."

Tried for Mersey Championship.

S. W. Roberts, the Warrington swimming crack, was a competitor in the swim for the championship of the Mersey on Wednesday. The distance was one mile, anti the race was swain near Liverpool. Roberts finished thirteenth, his time being 26min. 15ser• H. Taylor, Hyde Seal, won in Omin. 2sec. Roberts is 17 years of age, and holds the championship shield of the W.S.C.

A Popular Footballer.

Laws, the diminutive wing three-quarter of the Whines Northern Union. team, who is always popular with Warrington followers of the game, has gone to live at Oldham, and is not expected to play for Widnes in the forthcoming season. Oldham are anxious to secure his services, but Widnes has fixed the transfr fee at .2250, and it is not expected Oldham- will ,pay that sum.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Heather Day for Warrington.

Warrington is to have a "White Heather Day" in future in aid Of 'local charities. A start with such a scheme is to be Made to-daY, when.real white heather from Scotland will be on sale at the charity bowling match at the Horse and Jockey green. A penny will buy a pretty sprig of the wild flower, which signifies the best of luck, and, if given to a young lady by a youthful gallant, implies a proposal of marriage.

Death of Mrs. E. Bailey.

Mrs. Emma Bailey, widow of Mr. John t. Bailey, a Manchester man, who was an author of considerable repute, • died at her residence, Frogliall-lane, Warrington, and was interred at Stretford on Tuesday afternoon. She had been in indifferent health for some time: Mrs. Bailey was mother-in-law to Dr. Hodgkinson, of Warrington, and came to live in the borough on the death of her husband, five years ago.

Court IVllssionary Honoured.

A presentation of an illuminated address and a cheque was made at Bootle Police Court on Saturday, to Mr. Laws, the Police Court Mis- sionary at Warrington, who, until recently, held .a similar position at Bootle. The Mayor of Bootle, in making the presentation, referred to the excellent services rendered by Mr. Laws dur- . . . . .

lug his eight years' stay in Bootie. Other mag- istrates, and Superintendent Leslie, for the police, also paid their tributes to the work of the Court Missionary.

A Lapsed Commitment.

At Warrington County Court on Monday, Mr. E. Aubrey White asked Judge Stanger for leave to issue a judgment summons in the case of a debtor who had been committed, but the com- mitment order having been allowed to lapse without the debtor having gone. to prison, he contended that there was nothing in the County Court Acts to prevent his application being granted. Judge Stanger replied that it was a matter for consideration, and he would give his decision at the sitting of the Court on the 9th of October.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of a Police Constable.

It was with much regret that his comrades in the Warrington Police Force learned on Thurs- day of the demise of P.C. Joseph, Tolley, who succumbed to an acute attack of pneumonia. He .had been ill only a short time and was recently engaged controlling the traffic on the bridge. P.C. Tolley joined the Warrington Force in 1902 and was a native of Orford. He was a.keen patron of the game of bowls and was a member of the Corporation team which. won the Works Competition two years ago. The funeral will take place on. Sunday at the 'Ceme- tery at two o'cloCk.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
Rescued from the Canal.

An exciting incident occurred in the Bridge- water Canal, near the Black Bear Bridge, Latch- ford, on Sunday. A woman, named Margaret Brooks, 46 years, wife of Edward Brooks, living at 55, Tanner's-lane, was seen, struggling in the water. Fortunately assistance was at hand, and the woman was rescued in a weakened state by Mr. John Small, postman, 2, Poacher's-lane; Mr. Joseph Lowndes, window cleaner, 34, Fred. erick-street, and a lamplighter, whose name was not given. The woman stated that she did not know how she got into the canal, and was re- moved to the Union Hospital by the police.

Preachers on Sunday.

The preachers in Warrington Churches on Sunday will be as follow :

Bold-street Wesleyan :—Morning, Mr. R. J. Moss; evening, Rev. H. Williamson.

Wycliffe Congregational, Bewsey-street : Morning and evening, Rev. George Slack, of Wakefield.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan :—Morning, Mr. Wright, evening, Rev. W. A. Beckett.

Baptist Church, Latchford :—Morning and evening, Rev. J. S. Chedburn. Baptist Church, Golborne-street :—Morning, Councillor James Shaw; evening, Mr. B. Wynne Jones.

Cairo-street Chapel :—Morning and evening, Rev. H. McLachlan, Home Missionary College, Manchester.

Christadelphians, Toft's Assembly Rooms, Bridge-street:—Evening, Mr. J. Calvert, Old- ham.

Deaths in 'Warrington.

The number of deaths in Warrington last week were 20. Curiously enough ten males and the same number of females died. The death- rate was 14.0, compared with 12.7 last year.
