THE EXAMINEtti SATURDAY, AUGUST 23, 1913.

The Bank Amalgamation.

A further step will be taken towards the amalgamation of the Warrington and Runcorn Savings Banks on 2nd September, when a meet- ing will be herd to pass a resolution authoris- ing the amalgamation scheme.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
Progressive Labour League.

The Progressive Labour League holds another of its interesting meetings at Bridge Foot on Monday, at 7.30 p.m. Mr. William Megden, Manchester, is the speaker, and the chairman will be Mr. E. Wynne Jones.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
Citizens' Temperance League.

This league, which is doing much useful propaganda work for the cause of temperance, will hold a meeting in Bewsey-street, near the Farmers' Stores, on Wednesday night, at 7.30. The speaker will be Mr. F. W. Hall, Liverpool, and Mr. W. Twiss will be chairman.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
The Late Miss E. M. Massy.

An important sale of substantial furniture, the property of the late Miss Emily Jane Massy, will be conducted by Messrs. T. Sutton and Sons, in 'conjunction with Messrs. Clive and Co., at Greenbank, Latchford, on Ist and 2nd September. There are, amongst the goods, some interesting oil paintings and water-colours. The goods will be on view on Saturday next, and catalogues can be obtained from the auctioneers.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Divers at tile Bridge.

Divers have been at work in the turgid waters of the Mersey at the Warrington Bridge this week. Part of their work has been to secure the coffer dams made in connection with the foundations of the second part of the bridge. It is understood that they further investigated complaints of barge-owners that some rock in the river bed was impgding navigation, and on their report there is alprobability steps Will be taken to remove the stones.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Childish Play Ends in Death.

George Evans (3 years and 11 months), son of Charles W. Evans, wiredrawer, 3, Forster- street, 'Warrington, was on 6th August playing beside the boiler containing boiling water, when he was badly scalded. He died this week in. the Infirmary from shock and the injuries sus- tained to his arm. At the inquest, held by Coroner Brighouse yesterday, the father said he had taken the child away from the boiler the same day and warned him not to go again. lie did return and met with the accident. - The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

Death of Mr. John W. Welsby. Mr. John W. Welsby, the eldest son of Mr. and Mrs. T. Welsby, .of 13, Froghall-lane, War- rington, died on Tuesday', after an illness ex- tending back to January. He was 27 'years of age, and was a young man of exceptional promise. Ile was educated at Lorne *House School, Frogltall-lane, and, has since he left school, been employed in the office of the Borough Treasurer. Mr. Welsby was well known and highly esteemed by all with whom lie came in contact. His activities in assisting to raise to a successful issue, the Million Penny Fund in aid of the Infirmary will be long re- membered. The interment took place at the Cemetery yesterday afternoon, the first part of the service being held at Holy Trinity Church. Amongst those present were representatives. from the Borough Treasurer's Office.

Mr. T. H. qarside, of Manchester, the speaker at the Progressive Labour League meeting at Bridge Foot on Monday evening, put quite a different complexion upon Mr. Harold Smith's great scheme to turn Warrington into a con- centration camp, like the Unionists pursued with the Boer women and children in the late war, where he intends to intern the women and children of Ulster, so that the men might fight in the Carson-cum-Smith rebellion. To the great delight of an audience, neany 2,C00 strong, Ar. Garside made comedy of the whole scheme, and it was doubtful if the "March Hares," bril- liant comedy as it is, ever furnished an audience with as much merriment.

Mr. W. H. SHERBURN, who presided, referred to the splendid work done by the Government in the recent session. Now lords, he said, were going to agricultural shows to, make, as lords liked to do, after-dinner speeches about the wickedness of the Government. It was evident the forthcoming land agitation was beginning to frighten them. (Applause.) They had heard a great deal recently in War- rington, said Mr. GARSIDE, about Ireland. Where was Ireland? (Laughter.) According to some Tories, it was a wee island anchored off the coast of England, that produced disreputable, lazy, loafing, whisky-drinking blackguards. But if there was a war on, would they say that of the Irish; did the Tories say it during the Boer War? No! They told Paddy that he was a splendid fellow, the pick of the Empire, who, alone, could • keep the British flag flying. (Laughter and applause.)

===== 0002601_19130823_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
" HIS NAME IS SMITH."

You have a member for Warrington, I believe, said Mr. Garside, whose name is Smith? (Loud laughter, and a Voice: 'More's the pity !") No, not " more's the pity," because now he is your member you know him; and if he was not your member you would not know him. (Great laughter and applause.) I believe his name is Mr. Harold Smith—(Voice "That's him !") and he is quite sure that Ireland— Ulster rather, —is going to be a thorn in the flesh of England. According to Mr. Harold Smith, there are only two good things to be found in Dublin—stout and the water from the Liffey. (Laughter.) I do not say that Mr. Harold Smith has said so; I can show you that he thinks so. He thinks every Boman Catholic is a sworn member of a society which is going to bring the Pope from Home to rule in Ireland. (Laughter.) The King will have, according to Mr. Smith, to take a back seat entirely, and every time you meet a priest in the streets of Warrington you may, according to Mr. Smith, be sure that priest has got a document in his pocket for the organising of armed rebellion in Ireland, or if he has not he is on his way to get the document. (Laugh- ter.)

===== 0002601_19130823_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
DUMMY KING; DUMMY GUNS.

How can the Pope become king of Ireland? }las not Ireland already got a king—Carson. (Laughter and applause.) And Carson has already armed his troops. (A Voice: "Aye, and wooden guns!")' Yes, only dummy guns, but dummy guns are good enough for a dummy king. (Laughter.) At any rate, dummy troops, dummy guns and, a dummy king are good enough for a dummy rebellion, in which Mr. Harold Smith plays a part closely connected with the commissariat department, for the troops will need food and water, and, if the truth be 'known, "a dropth av whisky !" (Great laughter.) You could not, get Ireland to fight

CIIOIR, BAND AND OFFICIALS.

The open-air musical festival promoted by the Working Men's Committee, in aid of the War- rington Infirmary, attracted a large gathering in Bank Park on Sunday, in spite of the fact that the weather was unusually dull and sun- less.

Under the baton of Mr. Payton this united body of vocalists sang some choruses and anthems with good-effect, having regard to the difficulty of properly rehearsing pieces in the performance of which so many loosely knit vocal contingents are concerned. The two anthems, "The radiant morn" (Faning) and "0 how amiable" (Woodward), were undoubtedly the pieces of which the massed vOices gave the test interpre- tation. But in these instances, and indeed in all the work of the choir, the difficulty of many listeners was in getting near enough to hear the softer parts, and the connective instrumental passages, for, it must be remembered, there were only a pianoforte ands a harmonium to help the vocalists—a very slight support even when the best is made of these instruments. In the bigger vocal items,—the choruses, "The heavens are telling," "And the glory," and "Hallelujah,"—it must be confessed that the ensemble, though by no means lacking in the qualities of precision, taste,.and a nice blending

of the parts, lacked tone volume and the back- ing that ought to have been given to them by some adequate rendering of the. orchestral parts. And one could not help wondering, in this connection, why the obvious combination of the band and the choir was not effected in order to attain this result. As an instance of the enhanced effect which such a joining of the forces would have brought about, one may re- call the grandeur of the tonal effect character- ising the opening hymn to the tune of the "Old Hundredth;" in which band, choir and audience alike enthusiastically participated. The items played by Messrs. Crosfield's Band, under the conductorship of Mr. J. G. Tay- lor, were performed with a finish and a bril- liance which denotes a high standard of tech- nique, and a fine artistic sense on the part of every member of it. Only by predicating such qualities could " Crosfleld's " have attained to Its present pre-eminent- position amongst t rgan- isations of its kind in the country. The

It was a happy idea to intersperse the music of a large mixed choir with that of a first-class brass band, in the giving of an alfresco enter- tainment, with the object of benefiting a deserv- ing institution, and Mr. Hampson, the hard- working hon. secretafy to the committee, and Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Sac., are to be con- gratulated on the very successful way in which they organised the project and carried out the various arrangements.

The choir, of some 120 voices, was composed of members of the following Nonconformist Church Choirs: Wycliffe Congregational, Bold- street Wesleyan, Bewsey-road Wesleyan, Legh- street Primitive Methodist, Brick-street Indepen- dent Methodist, and the Working Men's Mission.

WARMED WARRINGTON NESTS.

Where is Irekind, then? Nobody can answer that better than Mr. Harold Smith, for he tells us a considerable portion of the population of Ireland is coining to Warrington. (Great laugh- ter.) You will all need votes for women on that question. (Renewed laughter.) There are, again I quote Mr. Smith, 500 nests warming for the poor women and children of Ulster in War- rington. (A Voice: "Nowt at t' Hall," and laughter.) They are going to be planted here, there, everywhere as shining lights and ex- amples of the tyranny, oppression and bigotry of the South of Ireland, and of the awful injus- tice perpetrated upon Ulster by this wicked Government, run by a still more wicked Welsh- man. (Laughter,) Let us drop joking, and come to actual fact. What does Mr. Harold Smith and his friends say? Well, they say the North of Ireland will never accept Home Rule, and that the English people do not want Home Rule. The Govern- ment has' been challenged to go to the country on the Home Rule Bill, and Mr. Asquith had asked the Unionists that if the verdict of the country was in favour of Home Rule, would the Unionists accept it. "No !" said Captain Craig. "Then why go to the country?" asked Mr. Asquith. The answer was "to turn, the Government out." And that is the true reason for the anti-Home Rule agitation. (Loud Iv-, plause.)

A SMITHONIAN PROVISO.

Mr. Garside went on to show how the Upion- ists, when in office, had misruled Ireland, and, through the aid of the police, had concocted bogus crimes and convicted innocent people for these bogus offences. Mr. Balfour had ad- mitted in the House of Commons that the police in the days of Unionist Government had done that, and how were they punished? A ser- geant was retired on a pension, two other officers were retired on an allowance of £2OO a year each, and three •other men, Mr. Balfour had referred to, were kept in the force. (A Voice: "Shame.")

Mr. Harold Smith, he added, has arranged for 500 women and children to be brought from Ulster to this locality in cases of need. That is a very safe proviso. (A Voice: "Put tilem in the brewery, and laughter.) Oh, no, no, not in the brewery. (Rene Wed laughter.) It is a very safe proviso. Let me say to Mr. Harold Smith, I have one or two vacant rooms in my house at Manches- ter, I have one or two vacant beds, I have a jolly large dining-table, and I can always spare food and clothing for those in need. Let me say to Mr. Smith, my house door shall be wide open If he requires to use it. Ah, but he won't re- quire to use it. (Laughter and applause.)

GENERAL GORDON'S CHALLENGE.

Let us grant he would require, and let us examine the facts. The late General Gordon went to Ireland during the famine, and he made this challenge in " The Times" afterwards: "I will give a thousand pounds to any absentee Irish landlord or 'his agent. who will live one week in anyone of :the cottages on the estates I have visited in the West of Galway." Did any of the Tory landlords accept? .oh, no, General Gordon still had his thousand pounds when he died. And you in Warrington will have your house room unoccupied by Ulster people when you die. (Laughter and applause.) I will not be any poorer, and you will not be any poorer by a loaf of bread, through Mr. Smith's scheme.

Don't trouble about ,that scheme, don't draw your money out of tTie Post Office Savings Bank, don't sell your motor car, there is no

need. (Laughter and cheers.) Let us see what the present outcry by the Tory party means.

DID NOT HELP STARVING IRISH.

Did tile Tory party open the doors of their stately nomes tO the starving Irish peasantry during the famine? They did not. The first ship load of provisions that arrived for the relief ol the peasantry came from New York, then a sixteen days voyage from Queenstown. It arrived in Ireland before a loaf of bread was sent from Liverpool. Did the Tories open their doors then? No!

To Mr. Harold Smith let me say: I challenge you to prove—l will meet you on. the platlorm Dere or anywhere else—l challenge you to prove mat any one solitary Tory door was opened in Warrington, or in England, to take in any man, woman, or little child evicted in Ireland by absentee landlords for the sake of rent. (Loud applause.)

The Tories can never lie. Froude was a Tory, and he tells us that, during the evictions, lie was made sick at heart by the bodies lying in the lanes and the women of Ireland trying to shelter their babies in the hedges; sick at heart seeing cottages with the roofs burned off and lying in ruined heaps of stone to drive the women and little children out, and no hearty sympathy nor aid was sent to these people by the Tories at that time. To-day these Tories come and ask for sympathy for rebels against the law. (Applause.)

THE COUNTRYSIDE

Discourse on 'Whales sad Seutvlost. and Fish that Live Deadt. HOLIDAY ECHOES.

Mr. Dunlop's article this week might apilm- priately be called "The Seaside," rather thaw' "The Countryside," but the holidays, whichi have reached the echo stage—unfortunately for most of us—must be held accountable for that. Besides, the natural history of land and sea overlaps; and no one knows better than does our contributor how to make the most of that Interesting fact.

A Captured Shrew.

One morning on Clydeside a friend brought In a live shrew, which he found on the course whilst golfing. The timorous, velvet-coated little beastie showed very little fear as we— there were four of us—handled it, and it ran quite fearlessly up and down our coat sleeves. After all the junior members of the family had examined, sympathised with, and petted it, we turned it down in the garden. But a young, half-grown Scotch terrier had been an inter- ested spectator of the little animal's movements, and evidently decided that it was fair " game " when we released it, as he instantly made after it. A timely interference, however, saved the shrew's skin, and it was assisted through the palings into the field on the other side, beyond, as we thought, the dog's reach. We watched the shrew running nimbly along a' weedy trench, and then lost sight of it for a time. By this time the puppy, not to be baulked of his game, had got through the garden gate, ran along a path up the hill, and made a wide detour through the field, when we found him slinking through the long grass, until he reached the spot where the shrew went through the palings. He now smelled and snkffed about until he found the trail, and before long he found the shrew, but we there to rescue again. When he found It, the shrew was doing its best to hide, with its long, sensitive nose buried at the roots of a tussock of grass, and it back arched. This time we took the dog away and placed the shrew beyond his further reach. It was very funny, though, to watch the native cunning of that little black dog. Sharks and Whales—and Bathers.

During the first week of August the Firth of Clyde waters sported quite an unusual assort- ment of the larger denizens of the deep. Por- poises playing and rolling in and out of the water in their gambols is such a common sight that few people, except holiday-makers from inland towns, take much notice of them. They are so common that they cease to be interesting to the natives. But when the cry of sharks in the estuary is set up, it becomes another matter,. for natives and visitors are alike on the alert. The first alarm was raised by a party of bathers, at Brodick, in the Island of Arran, who saw a couple of these sea tigers approach- ing. A number of boatmen went out with guns, but no further evidence was seen of the sharks, and folks became sceptical, and pooh- poohed the idea, but visitors, all the same, did not venture to bathe in the bay any more that week. Further evidence of their presence was furnished next day by the crew of a yacht tak- ing part in the regatta on Loch Fyne reporting having seen a large shark basking in the sun on the surface of the water, his dorsal fin sticking up like a tiny lugsail. Following the sinister-looking fin were dozens of screaming gulls. Two or three days later positive proof of the Brodick story was supplied by the cap- ture of three half-grown sharks by herring fishermen at V Corrygills, only a short distance from Brodick. The largest measured four feet ten inches, and the beasts, as they lay on the -beach, were visited by large numbers of excited visitors. The old ferryman at Arden- tinny-told me, in a talk about sharks, that they are attracted by the large quantities of salmon in the firth during the hot weather. During periods, of droughts in mid-summer the rivers are very low and the salmon are unable to ascend to their usual Nols. I saw a shark in the Bute Museum over five feet long, taken near Rothesay two years ago. No wonder their appearance strikes terror in the mind or for a sight of those sharp, saw-like, gleaming rows of teeth are calculated to frighten the bravest. A sailor friend once told me that the most distressing sight he had ever seen was that of a shipmate who had fallen from the yards into the sea being dragged under water by the leg, in the vice-like grip of the teeth of a big shark.

There is a small school of Bottle-nosed Whales in Clyde Waters at present. Individuals have been seen repeatedly in different parts of the estuary. Two or three were seen in Loch Fyne following the herring. Another was seen the same day at Hunter's Quay, the yachting head- quarters. When he rose to the surface tO "blow," the noise was such that four holiday- makers were nearly frightened out of their wits, and they rowed inshore in all haste. The pre- sence of huge shoals of mackerel and herrings Is said to be responsible for these big visitors. The Necessary Pinch of Salt. Some of the Nature observations recorded in the public Press these hot days need to be accepted with a very generous pinch of salt. For example, a fanciful yarn about a seal comes from Islay. The narrative sitys that a female seal and her young were sporting among the rocks, and one of the little ones, having gone on to a projecting rock to rest, fell into a rock pool on the other side.- The distress of parent and young was painful and the rocks resounded with their cries. A gamekeeper, who.chanced to pass (like the Samaritan of old) that way, lifted the young seal and threw it back into the. sea. The mother no sooner had it in her pos- session again than, it is alleged, "she took it under her left fin, while, with the right, she- adwinistered a good, sound spanking, the cries of The young one being very human." I told this story to a small boy of my acquaintance. All the appreciation I received was one word— " Garn 1" Scotch Humour and the Heat.

The favourite pastime in the evening al the Clyde watering-places is to hire a boat, lines, and bait, and go out about half-a-mile from the shore and fish. Quite a nice haul can be made for the morning's breakfast in this way. I heard of an Ayrshire man who had been thus

engaged, on landing, placed his catch and tackle on the beach for a moment, when one of the fish gave a last flutter with its tail. Ob- serving this, he quite innocently said : " Fluckies (flounders) live a long while efter they're deid." Yet they say the Scots race is devoid of humour. It must be the heat! . Speaking of heat reminds me—just before I lay my pen down—of a young Scots laddie who was asked how he knew when it was summer in Scotland. "When the tar on the tram- lines is soft," he replied. The temperature, according to this test, may he taken any day on the floor of the approaches to the new War- rington Bridge.

G. A. DUNLOP.

P.S.—Since I wrote the above, a shark, 13ft. long and "twice the thickness of a man's body," has lieen caught in the herring nets off the Ayrshire coast. - G. A. D.

SUFFRAGE NOTES.

Guarding the Maternity Benefit

for the Women.

TO COMBAT VICE.

Specially Contributed.

The victory won in the House of Commons on August 6th marks an era in the position. of women. The maternity benefit is to be given to the mother, and this is an admission of the economic importance of the wife's work in the home. It had been decided in Committee that the maternity benefit is the mother's benefit, but an amendment was proposed (that the re- ceipt of the wife or husband should be a suffi- cient discharge to the society making the pay- ment), which would practically have reversed the decision of the Committee. Lord Robert Cecil quoted the Glasgow Medical Officer of Health as saying: "Abuse of the benefit money is, unhappily, very common.- CaSes are num- erous in. which the husband receives the entire 30s. and spends it on liquor. It is obvious, from the above statement, that in many Oases abuse of the money would le prevented by direct payment of the benefit to the wife." Though the amendment was carried, the posi- tion was saved by Lord Robert Cecil, who moved to add to the clause making the receipt .of the husband a sufficient discharge, the words "if authorised by the wife."

Besides the deputations to Mr. McKenna and Mr. Asquith, the Suffragist members of the Government also received a 'deputation from the National Union of Women's Suffrage. Societies. Mrs. Fawcett and Miss Eleanor Rathbone also had an interview with Mr. Bonar Law. The proceedings in both cases were private. "

AWAITING A COLTIAGEOUS STATESMAN.

The Bishop of Lincohilwrites in the ",Times": "The demand of women for the vote has come to be one of the greatest moral and social movements of our time. It gathers strength and volume daily; all the forces of progress are working in its favour; it cannot be set aside. It. ,Only awaits the handling of a sympathetic, courageous and constructive statesman, who has Imagination to conceive of the England that is to be. Methods of repression, like the Cat and Mouse Act would deserve only ridicule, were it not for the suffei;ings they involve. The only sane and lasting remedy for the present discontent is truly Liberal legislation; that is, the extension of liberty through the;franchie."•

===== 0002601_19130823_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
A HIDEOUS SCOURGE.

For years past Suffragists have been urging the necessity for breaking through the conspir- acy of silence with regard to the -results of -Ml- morality, which has led to the -sacrifice of thousands of innocent women and children, through what an eminent-member of the Inter- national Medical Congress described, as a " devastating pestilence, second only to tuber- culosis and alcohol in its effectS upon the race." They :pointed, out that so long as the public were in ignorance of the .prevalence of this disease, and of its highly contagious char- acter, little could be done to check its terrible ravages. Their agitation for a full discussion of the question was Met by a campaign of abuse and misrepresentation. They were accused of prurience, of being devoid both of modesty and reason.

Far from revelling in disgusting details, their attitude on this question was to the leaders of the Suffrage movement a most painful duty, which they could only bring themselves to face by the knowledge that the welfare of the race was at stake. But now they are not alone in the battle. Eminent members of the medical profession are loudly .proclaiming that know- ledge must displace ignorance. "This hideous scourge," said one of the speakers, "costs the country millions of pounds, fills many homes with preventable misery, overcrowds our workhouses, prisons and luna- tic asylums with imbeciles, idiots, criminals, epileptics, and other insane persons, 'saps the vigour of the nation, and, if uncontrolled, may eventually endanger its very existence.' In this country alone there are some 500,000 fresh victims every year Sir Maleohn Morris pointed out that a move- ment was on foot to indUce the British Govern- ment to take the question-up; and ,since then Mr. Asquith has announced in Parliament that the Government have agreed to institute an inquiry lit-119 the causes and treatment of venereal disease.

"In this country," said Sir Malcolm Morris. " the State insisted on the notification of infec- tious diseases, took charge of the insane, built fever hospitals, 'rigidly inspected factories and workshops, and in other ways stretched out its long arm to protect the health of the people, yet it ,did not lift a finger against these con- tagious diseases, which were more ruthless than the Destroying Angel which slew the first born. Words failed to express his sense of the grossness of such incongruity."

. - The whole tone of the discussion was ex- tremely high. Far from being actuated by the spirit of the " C.D. Act" (which sought to make vice safe for men by police control of women) the Congress was desirous of combating not only the effects of evil living, but vice itself./ Many speakers insisted on the need for the

Continued in next column.

greater protection of girls and destitute chit- dren,, and the rigid suppressipn of souteneurs ; others on the education of boys in the dangers of laxity and the duties of self-control. A paper by Major H. C. French laid great stress upon moral and religious considerations as a check upon the young, and several other speakers insisted that the question was asocial, as well as a medical problem. It is a problem that can only be solved by men and women working together with mutual trust and respect, and the good of the race steadily before their eyes.

E. L. B.

Temperance and Its Workers.

Sir,—ln your correspondence column of the 16th inst. - there is a letter signed by lb A Templar." lam pleased to 'see there are Others who admit that the Temperance workers of this town need waking up, and I heartily thank him for his cordial invitation to join their Lodge at Buckley-street. But let me state that I was' perfectly aware that such meetings were being held in the various schools of this town. It was not at such good and useful societies as he mentions that I was aiming in my letter of July 21st. . The good work that the-Sons of Temperance, Good Templars and the Recha- bites have done, and are still doing, all over the country, proves their usefulness to the Tem- perance cause, and, most of all; to our rising generation. I have personally attended their different Lodges as a speaker, but since I became a worker in the Temperance cause I have devoted Myself to saving the children from ever partaking of this strong drink by teaching and demonstrating to them the injurious effects of tasting it. My letter was aimed at those Temperance workers in connection with the various Bands of Hope in Warrington. In Yorkshire and East Lancashire we can get good attendances at the Band of Hope Meetings, and hold them every week (winter and summer), and I do not see why it cannot be done in this town. I say all honour to those societies I have mentioned for their work to save the coming generation from the evils of the drink traffic, but I am prepared to prove that if other Temperance workers in connection with either of these societies, and especially Band of Hope •Societies,-will give my suggestion a trial this coming winter, great progress cah be Made. As I. stated in my pre- vious letter, if I can do anything in the way of counsel or advice, and, what is most needed by. all of us. good, practical work, then I do not care what time or expense I am put to. Noth- ing will be too much for me to do in this glorious work, and if, as your correspondent suggests, I can be of any use to their society, in advising, I can only say that I give you, sir,.full privilege to give him my private address, and then he can sec me personally, or write me, to make an appointment any evening. To all Temnerance workers I would quote the follcPw- ing lines : Let us go forth and fight in this conflict, Shielding our children, every one, From the drunkard's cup of sorrow, As we labour on, labour on. A REFORMED DRUNKARD.

The " Examiaer " and Liberalism.

Sir,—! nolic,-,! with pleasure the great im- provement in your valuable paper, 'commencing; with' August end, 1913, issue. I wish to ex- press my gratification, and to say how pleased I was to. note the splendid advance made, which should, without doubt, increase its popularity and enlarge its circulation. Many have (ex- pressed themselves highly pleased with the paper and its contents. The " Examinpr's " past has been very hon- ourable; it has never tried. all sorts of methods to hide its true colours. I have read the paper for nearly 40 years, and I am proud to say it has always been Liberal, and has proved itself a living force in the.town, ,There being -a great preponderance of Lib- erals and Progressives in Warrington, further expansion may be looked for with confidence. I am proud to remember my first .vote. It was in the 1880 election, and at every election since I have voted for the best candidate, but not always with the same result.' I have noted this—that you are very consid- erate with some of your correspondents, who, like Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, have left the Liberal party, but who are everlastingly saying they are Liberals, and who think they are possessed of - a kind of -Superior knowledge. Some of them go on the need of change in our "Fiscal , System," "National Service," - or. "Welsh Disestablishment,"-and'Some champion the Suffragettes. I have noticed that not many. letters are written agAinst, Home Rule, so it may be concluded that Home Rule has now ceased to act on people's nerves. The correspondents to whom I have referred are merely wreckers, like their leader, "Joey." This. Government was elected to pass mea- sures for' the benefit of the masses, and I. am glad . they have been so successful. Let them go on without fear either of the wobblers,,or the recognised Opposition, who were .such miserable- failures for the 20 years previous to 1906.

Congratulating you again upon the great improvement in the "Examiner." J. Fir. HEYWOOD.

Pickmere-street.

[The above is one of Many similar letters we, have received. We thank Mr. Heywood, and the other correspondents, who have written in flattering terms, most sincerely. The sug- gestions they make shall receive every attention.—THE EDITOR.)

Local Veto and Local Option.

Sir,—! have read Mr. Leslie's letter and am quite satisfied that he did not mean to ,accuse me of wilfully misrepresenting facts. With reference to the question of Local Option, Mr. Leslie submits that :the obvious fiittiningof C:lause2 of thelicensing Bill of 1908 is that the vote of the -Majority in an area is to be an instruction to the magistrates to refuse or to grant a ,licence. According to the Bill, the vote of the majority in favour of a pro- hibitory resolution would certainly be an in- struction to the magistrates to refuse a licence, an instruction the magistrates would be bound to obey; but, on the other hand, the vote of the majority against a prohibitory resolution would have no such compelling force, and it is quite. conceivable that in many cases, in spite of a majority vote against 'prohibition, the magis- trates would refuse to grant a licence. A majority in a district in -favour :of a licenct-i ,would therefore -be -impotent. With Mr. Leslie's own view of Local Option, I heartily agree, but it is somewhat different from the proposals of the Licensing Bill of 1908 and from the generally accepted views of the leading temperance organisations. The view that the majority should decide for " licence " or "no . licence" is popular, reasonable and democratic, but, according to Mr. Joseph Matins, of the National Temperance Federation, "While England is England nothing so in- famous will ever be offered them by any British Government."

• What is asked for by temperance reformers. and proposed in the Licensing Bill of 1908 is really Local Veto, an unreasonable and un- democratic thing. . EDW. TUCKER. , August 21st, 1913.

Languor and Depression

Has it ever occurred to you that the feeling of languor and depression which sometimes makes you feel miserable is due to stomach and liver troubles ? It pro- bably is, for even slight indigestion„ or faulty 'action of the liver certainly does produce:.depressicin, fatigue, and weariness. At such times it Is 'clear that the remedy you need is one ti:at W!!! stomach, -liver, and bowels to regtilar healthy action. Mother Seigel's Syrup does that most effectively. For over forty years people all ovef the world lia7e found it to be a splendid and a ready means ci banishing and preventing digestive troubles, whether arising from indigestion, a sluggish state of the liver, or inactive bowels.

"Yes, the farmers will make money out of the oats," replied the visitor. "Oats?" queried the lady, "1 was speaking about the corn on my feet."

The holiday spirit which so pronouncedly marked last week, has vanished this week—it's work, work, and work again. Still the villagers hold pleasant recollections of the vacation, and some still imagine they are by the sad sea wave's. \

One of the "nuts" to heighten the imaginary effects, proudly strutted through the village in a yachting cap. Quite naty-cal," you know.

The Petty Sessions have been held this week. Wednesday's court did not produce anything sensational. You see, we are so very, very good folk here. The magistrates on the bench were Messrs. T. F. Ridgway (in the chair), and Messrs. S. Davies and S. Gould. Their's was not hard labour, nor did they need to give anyone hard labour.

Doa COLLAR VANISHES.—Dog collars are often stolen. This fact was once more brought to light at Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, when Ernest Whitlow, Heatley Heath Farm, Heatley, was summoned for allowing his dog to be at large without a collar.—Defendant said the dog had a collar when it left home. It was not the first time he had had the dog's collar stolen. —A fine of 2s. 6d. wass imposed.

CYCLING ON FOOTPATEe:—At Lymni Petty Sessions on Wednesday, two colliers, Aaraed James Jones and Joseph Thorpe, hailing from SWinton, were each fined 2s. 6d. and costs for riding their cycles on the footpath at Partingtou.—The police officer stated that there was a properly defined footpath at the place where he saw the defend- ants. The road was paved with setts, but the cyclists preferred the footpath. ALARMING ACCIDENT.

Horse, Cart and Driver Fall in Disused Quarry. The disused stone quarry known as the Delph was the scene of a peculiar accident on Thurs- day morning. • The quarry- has for some time been used as a ref use tip, and it was in this connection that the accident took place. One of the carts belonging to the Urban Dis- trict Council had just been backed to the edge of the quarry,, preparatory to its load being tipped, when a sudden.. gust of wind blew a piece of paper before the horse's eyes. The animal at once plungetl backwards, with the result that cart, horse and driver, William Davies, who had gripped the bridle, all tumbled into the hollow. Some men who happened to be working in the vicinity at once went for help, and in a short time ample assistance was on the spot. Neither the man or the horse was much hurt, but the harness and cart shaft had to be cut to liberate the animal.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
A DISASTROUS DAY FOR CYCLISTS.

Sunday appears to have been quite a disas- trous time for cyclists in this district, for in addition to the sight of quite an unusual num- ber of persons utilising their repair outfits for punctures, &c., there occurred two nasty acci- dents on the Church Brow. At about 345 in the afternoon a motor cyclist started his machine down the incline by the Parish Church lychgate. Unluckily, he could not see anything that was approaching from the opposite direc- tion, and as he ran out into the main road he collided with another motor 'cycle coming from Broom Edge. Mr. James Hewitt, of Lynam, who passed the spot immediately- before the accident happened, saw what was likely to take place, and did his best to warn the cyclists. Fortunately, he attracted the attention of the motorist coming from Penny Hill, who shut off his' engine • just as the other man dashed into him. Neither rider was hurt much, beyond shock, but the Motor cycles were damaged. Within a very few minutes another accident occurred a little rower -down the hill. As a cyclist, closely followed by a motor car, was proceeding, down the hill, a second cyclist passed the car from behind and then crossed over in front to his correct side. This manoeuvre prevented the driver of the motor from passing the first cyclist, whom he had just reached, and so, with the object of avoiding a disaster, he ran' his car into the wall, but it also, unfortunately, struck the cyclist, who was thrown from his machine and sustained injuries to one arm, and a, severe. shock. The bicycle was smashed, a front. wheel. .of the car damaged and the wind screen broken.

TRAPPED BY MARKED COINS.

Robbing of Slot Meters.,

At the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, a youth, named Fred Jennings, of Moston-street, was. charged with stealing, on the 27th of July, eightpence from a slot gas meter at the works of Moston and Company, the monies of the Lymm Urban District Council, and, further, with wil- fully damaging the meter to the extent of 49. Mr. Mullard, clerk to the Lymm Council, said that this was the first prosecution the Council had undertaken. Of late there had been a number of cases of robberies from slot meters, and they hoped by bringing a case before the magistrates that the thefts would be stopped. Walter Donaldson, gas manager, stated that he examined the meter on the 25th of July, and it was then in good order. Three days later he found the lock had been broken and the money missing. When he visited the works on July 22nd he marked certain coins and placed them in the box, but these were missing. . Francis Bacre, an employe at the works, said the youth was left on his own in the works, with instructions to sweep out the office. On wit- ness's return he noticed that the lock of the gas meter had been broken off, and it was in

'447

proper order when he left the premises. Mr. William Adams, manager of the works, said in consequence of meter robberies his sus- picions fell upon the defendant, and he decided to lay a trap to detect him. The defendant was left in the office, with instructions to sweep it. The meter was then intact, but on witness's return he noticed the lock had been broken and the money taken, including the three marked pennies. He called at the house of the accused and asked him if he had any coppers in his pocket, and he handed to witness a number of coins, including the three marked pennies which had been plaoed in the meter. Mr. MuHard said that when he was called to the office accused made use of the remark: "My God! It is a ' bait ' for me."

Pefendant denied that he made use of the remark.

The Chairman said they looked upon it as a very serious case, and they regretted to see a young mq.n in such a position. Ile would have to pay ss. for the first offence and 16s. 6d. for the damage to the meter.

STOCKTON HEATH

TOWNSHIP TITTLE-TATTLE.

The people of Mars will shortly be puzzled. They will see a new illuminated spot on the face of the earth.. Stockton Heath Parochial Committee will he responsible for this.

They are going to erect a new lamp at Lumb Brook. We used to go to a brewery for beer. Now we are going to go for water---when fire's about. ThQ Brewery Brigade is to be asked to fight the Heathen fires.

Why the Brewery fire fighters? most people will be inclined to ask. They have outbid Warrington for the job and have got it right enough. Warrington wanted £75 a year; the Brewery is to do the work for "nowt."

The cautious Heathens, of course, have lc, opted the cheapest tender—" nowt."

Lawrenson's Brow wants to be made more respectable—outwardly. A triangular patch is to be put into a state of respectability, and the District Council are asked to do it.

Truly we are rushing ahead of the times, that we are in Stockton Heath.

The Parochial .Committee " wisibly " swelled with indignation at the County Authority. They asked for a policeman to be kept On duty at the Police Station night and day. The Cheshire County Council have now stated that such is not necessary. And the committee are angry at the rebuff. Their next step is a dark secret. "What is the quickest way to get to the train terminus?" asked a visitor to the Heath.

"Run !" said the schoolboy Heathen. Then seeing the visitor's eyes flash fire he promptly ran.

A possible source of infection among children was hinted at by Mr. McDermid at the Paro- dual meeting on Tuesday, when he asked where the refuse from the manholes was tipped. Mr. Bottomley, ever on the alert for possible dangers, moved that an inquiry be made, to which the Committee at once agreed.

PAVING OF THE RoAns.—The five years' guaran- tee by the contractors to maintain the roads they laid down loeing about to expire, the Parochial Committee on Tuesday night decided to ask the contractors, the Northern Quarries' ComPany, to effect ;a few minor repairs to the roads they made inl9oB. It was stated that the roads made by this company had worn very well. The meeting decided to ask the firm to effect the necessary reuairs. An' offer from the company to maintain the roads at the rate of a penny per superficial yard per annum for the next five years was sent to the District Council for con- sideration.

NOT GREENWICH TIBIE.—A Complaint having been made about .the street lamps being extin- guished too early, a letter was read from the lighting authority at a meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee on Tuesday that it was not so; they extinguished according to Greenwich time.—Mr. Whitley contended that the lamps were'put out before the time, and he estimated that they were paying for gas they did not get.—Mr. C. Hallows: I have seen the lamps alight at 11.30 in the morning. (Laughter.)—The Chairman (Mr. Pilling) added that evidently there was a divergency between the Greenwich time of the Gasworks and the time filed by Mr. Whitley.—The latter retorted that he took the time from a local year book and was surprised that the book was not correct. —The matter dropped, as it was understood the Gas Authority would look into the matter.

WHEN THERE'S A CONSTABLE ABOUT. There was a little amusing discussion at the meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Com- mittewon Tuesday night—Mr. G. Pilling presid- ing—ow the question of police attendance at the Police Station. A letter was read from the Clerk of the Peace for Cheshire, stating that the Standing Joint Committee, who control the police, having heard the Chief Constable's views, did not consider it necessary, for the pre-, sent, to meet the request of the committee that a constable should be on duty at the Police Station at Stockton Heath, day and night. Mr. Bottomley : That is the treatment you get from the governing body at Chester. The Chairman: es; that has put a " stopper " on it. (Laughter.) Mr. Clarke: In the course of the discussion the County Authorities gave an indication that al- though a policeman is not constantly in the station there is one not very far away. It is, of course, not for the police to tell us where the Men are. Mr. Bottomley : Well, experience tells me that they are not easily to he found. (Laughter) Mr. Whitley: Since we have had extra ,police there is always one in the vicinity of the Square. I Mr. Mcllermid : They ought to be near the Station much more.

Mr. Clarke: They would be within hearing of the fire bell.

A VILLAGE "BEAUTY SPOT."

Should the triangular plot at La.wrenson's Brow be converted, or not, into a beauty spot? was the question raised at Tuesday's meeting of the Parochial Committee, and now the Runcorn District Council will have to decide.

The Surveyor (Mr. J. Swinton) reported that although the plot was of no particular advan- tage to the public, except that upon it stood a gas lamp and a signpost, he had sent a man to cut the grass and make the spot look a little more tidy. Mr: G. Pilling (the chairman) said the people in that district desired to have the plot enclosed. "I think they are inclined to pay the cost of making it a little bit more respectable," he added. " The corner, is certainly in a very rough place at present" Mr. J. Hallows: There 'would be no objection if they paid for it. The Chairman: It is hardly fair they should bear the expense when the site belongs to the District Council. Mr. Whitley: If the gas limp .*as removed it would certainly be a great improvement. The Chairman: It is. a rough place e.s_it is.

Mr. W.hitley Yes; they tip rubbish there. -Mr. Mclntyre: Leave it as it is and let the children have theLndvantage of it. _

_ Mr. Rose: No; the place is not in keeping with the surroundings. • Mr. Whitley proposed, and Mr. .T. .11aliow3 seconded, that the District Council be isketh to put the plot in order.—Agreed.

DANGER AT THE CORNER.

Stockton Heath, like most places, has its "most dangerous corner," and at Tuesday's meeting of the Parochial Committee it was alleged that this spot at Lumb Brook was ren- dered still more dangerous by deficient lighting. Accidents had happened at the spot, said Mr. J. Hallows. A few weeks ago, he said, an old lady, accompanied by her son, was approaching the corner, when a cyclist ran into her. She had to be taken into a house in the neighbour- hood, and attended to before she could proceed home. • On another occasion a man collided with the railing, and was thrown into the brook. A lamp ought certainly to be provided. He had pre- viously raised the question, and, if action were not talen, there. might be serious cases.

been erected before at the place. Mr. Whitley: This matter has been before us a considerable time, and it was arranged we should write to the Corporation Gas Committee, and we did so. They said they would put a lamp there if, we paid half the expense of laying the main.

PROTECTION FROM FIRE.

Agreement with the Brewery for

Assistance.

WARRINGTON OFFER REJECTED.

At the monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee, the proposed methods of dealing with fire were again discussed. The proposal of the Warrington Corporation to place the resources of the borough at the dis- posal of the village .for £75 a year was rejected; and the offer of the Wilderspool Brewery Bri- gade to remain as heretofore open to calls to lires within a reasonable distance of Wilders- riool—including Stockton Heath, but •excepting Warrington, was accepted.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SUCCESSFUL PROPOSAL

The proposals of the Wilderspool Brewery Co. were that they would continue to place their ,voluntary brigade at the disposal .of the village, as reported at a meeting of the committee some months ago. Telephonic communication was to be established with the lodge at the brewery, in order that intimation of an outbreak of fire might be promptly conveyed to the brigade at any hour, the Parochial Committee to pay part of the cost of installation. There would be some one at the Brewery to receive messages.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CORPORATION TERMS.'

The following were the terms offered by the *Corporation : Town Clerk's Office, Watrington; 13th August, 1913. Dear Sir,—Referring to your letter of the 2nd July last, I am directed by the Fire Engine Committee to state that they will be prepared to attend outbreaks of fire in the parish of Stock- ton Heath, on the following terms and condi- tions :- 1. An annual payment of 0t75 to be made by the parish of Stockton Heath to the Cor- poration. This sum to include out-of-pocket expenses, and the Parish Council to recoup themselves by charging insurance companies and others for fire expenses in their district. 2. The Parish Council to provide and maintain any fire alarm which they consider necessary. 3. Tne motor fire engine, when delivered, in addition to the steam engines and appli- ances, to be available for extinguishing fires in the parish of Stockton Heath. 4. At least one practice per annum to be held in the parish. 5. The rule as to attendance of men at country fires, in so far as attendance in Stockton Heath is concerned, not to apply. 6. The Parish Council to be liable for any claim which may arise in respect of the abstraction by the fire brigade of any water from any pool, canal or stream, in or ad- joining the parish. -7. The Corporation to have first call on the services of the fire brigade,- fire engines and appliances, for use in the boroagh. B.. The above terms to be subject to re- vision every three years. Fours faithfully, J. LYON ' :WHITTLE.

MR. BOTTOMLEY'S INEFFECTUAL PROTEST.

Mr. J. McDermid having proposed and Mr. J. Hallows seconded the adoption of t lie 6l1:.) coin- mitteces report, :Mr. A. Bottomley moved an amendment that the agreement with Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. be not entertained; that they enter into an agreement with the Warrington Cor- poration on the terms offered; and that the Stockton Heath Fire Brigade be dispensed with altogether. He said he did not think that they as a public body• were doing right in entering into an agreement with a private firm. The 'committee would have 'no -locus standi, hut would be absolutely at the mercy of the firm. He agreed that Messrs. Greenall, Whit- ley and Co. had always been good to the parish, but they had stated in their letter to the committee that they would not enter into any binding agreement. If the committee were able to make a binding agreement, particularly with a corporation, on such a question, it was their duty to do so. Under the conditions proposed by the Corpora- tion; Stockton Heath would have the right to the services of the brigade at all .times, except on those rare occasions when a fire in the parish happened at the same time as one in the borough. If a fire occurred at any other time and the brigade did not turn out, the com- mittee would be able to take action. With such terms before them they- ought to dispense with thO village brigade. It was not good enough ' for that district. The question of ot s. d. ought ' not to enter into their consideration, though the terms proposed by the Corporation were very reasonable, and they ought to accept such a splendid offer and arrangement. A public body in an important and thickly-populated dis- trict like Stockton Heath ought not to be sub- servient in the hands of others. 'there was no seconder of the amendment. Mr. McDermid We ought to express some opinion_pn _ the matter?

-The Chairman (Mr. G. Pilling) : I cannot allow that unless the amendment is seconded. Mr. MeDermid : It is of interest to everybody. The recommendation of the Fire Appliances Committee was carried, Mr. Bottomley being the only one to vote against it. The Clerk (to Mr. Bottomley): Would you like your vote 'recorded? THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 23, 1913.

THE WORLD OF LETTERS.

New Books.

A History of Freedom of Thought.

By Prof. J. B. Bury. Ancient Art and IliMal.

Williams and Nor-

By Dr. Jane Harrison. gate, London. The series of volumes published by Messrs Williams and Norgate under the title of 11tome University Library of M4lern Knowledge," comprises seventy-five volumes on a great variety of subjects, social, scientific, philosoplii- cal and literary. They have been written' by men of distinguished ability and of recognised authority on the subjects of which they treat. The latest additions to the library are 'A His- tory of Freedom of Thought," by Professor J. B. Bury, of Cambridge, and "Ancient Art and Ritual?' by Dr. Jane E. Harrison. Professor Bury's work is of more than ordin- ary interest. He points out that whilst thought is free, this freedom is Of little value if the thoughts may not be expressed and communi- cated to others. He traces the history, of the conflict between Authority and Reason, and en- deavours to show that free and unfettered as it was in the earlier times of Greece and Rome, the adoption of Christianity by Constantine the Great, which resulted in Christianity becoming the dominant creed, led to the enchaining of reason and the destruction of liberty of thought. He points out that during the Middle Ages re- ligious and political authority were supreme, and ruthlessly crushed out reason and freedom of thought and speech. Witii the Renaissance there came a revolt. against, this bondage; which has' widened and deepened and developed into the rationalism Of :this twentieth century. One may not agree with all the views expressed by Prof. Bury, but the reader will admit that the subject is treated with great 'ability and. strict Impartiality, such as to make this volume a valuable addition to the library. Dr. Jane Harrison's book, " Ancierlt Art and Ritual," is written in a different style, hut with equal ability. One is struck with the assured convictions of the writer, and the persuasive, if commanding manner in which she expresses therm You may not agree with them all, but it is a pleasure to read them. In the beginning she seeks' to show that Xrt sprang from ancient ritual, notably the ritual of the Greek Drama. Later in the book she deals with Art in modern times, and discusses its nature and its relation to religion and morality. These later chapters are most interesting, and the whole volume is of value not only to the artistic world, but to the general reader, who can appreciate clear- ness of thought expressed in forcible and fascinating language.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
National Life and Character: A Forecast. By C. H. Pearson. Macmillan and Co., Ltd. ss.

This is a new impression of a book published by. the late Dr. Charles Pearson in 1893, and we are, perhaps, in a better position to judge of his predictioN and generalisations now than on its first issue. Much has happened in the twenty years that have elapsed since that time to confirm apparently many of Dr. Pearson's fears. But much also has happened, we think, to render some of these fears groundless. Dr. Pearson surveys the world and sees the white man everywhere supplanted by the black and yellow races, save in Europe, Canada and the .Northern States of America. The fertility of the white man is less than that of all other races; therefore in point of numbers wherever he 'colonises, he is overwhelmed by the natlye races, and rhY black or yellow immigrants. In the political and industrial development of China he sees .great dangers for the future of the European peoples. China is certain lo enter into competition with Europe and America in the markets of the world, and as a .consequence will become a formidable military power, as JaPan' has already' becorrie: - And this competition kvill not be between nations on an equal footing industrially, but between nations on the one hand with a fairly high standard of life and comfort, and on the other a nation with an enormous and ever-increasing population, and a comparatively low standard of life. In Dr. Pearson's opinion, the outcome of this industrial struggle is plain--the Euro- pean cannot hold his .own. • In his forecast of increasing armaments, Dr. Pearson was perhaps more correct than' be thought, although England has not yet adopted conscription. In the field of home politics, the tendency, according to our author, is 'everywhere in pc direction of State Socialism. And this will dis- courage invention, and eventually bring about a general deterioration of character. The pessi- mistic note running through the book may be judged froth Dr. Pearson's concluding pas- sage:--

it is now more than probable that our science, our civilisation, our great and real advance in the practice of government, are only bringing us nearer to the day when the lower races will predominate in the world, when the higher races will lose their noblest elements, when we shall ask nothing from the day but to live, nor from the future but that we may not deteriorate. Even so, there will still remain to us ourselves. Simply to do our work in life, and to abide the issue, if we stand erect before the eternal calm as cheerfully as our fathers faced the eternal unrest, may be nobler training for our souls- than the faith in progress. Although Dr. Pearson concludes with this dismal picture of our future. and although we may dissent from many of his conclusions, we may cordially commend his book to the serious attention, of our readers. There is. food for deep. and anxious' thought in this book, and it is well that it .should be brought within the reach of a new and larger circle of readers.

NEW SHILLING THEOLOGICAL LIBRARY. Messrs. Macmillan and Co. have published a series of theological books by well-known writers, in a form which will bring them within the reach of those readers, whether clerical or lay, to whom they are not readily accessible in their present. form. The series includ4 a few -books such as " Ecce Homo" and Archdeacon Farrar's "Eternal Hope" and "Seekers after 'God," which have already been issued at the popular prie of is. net. But to these are. added a dozen other volumes by such writers hs Charles Kingsley, Dean Church, Bishop Westcott, Dr. Hort and Dr. Phillips' Brooks. who • belong to a past generation, and by more modern writers. • such as Dr. Illingworth, the Rev. William Temple, the author of "Pro Christ() et Ecclesia," , and the Rev. Bernard Lucas, ,whose volume, "The Faith of a Christian," was in the first in- stance published anonymously. , The twelve books published are: "The Candle of the'Lord and nine other Sermons," by Bishop Phillips Brooks; "Sixteen Village Sermons," by Dean Church;• "Discipline of the Christian Character," by Dean Cliurch ; "Sermons on the Books Of the Bible," by the Rev. Professor Hort ; "Personality, Human and Divine," and "Divine Immanence," by the Rev. Dr. Ming- worth; "Village Sermons" and "Good News of God: Twenty Sermons," by Charles Kings- ley: "The Faith of a Christian," by the Bev. Bernard Lucas; "The Faith and Modern Thought," by the Rev. William Temple; "The Gospel of the Resurrection," by Bishop West- cott ; and "Pro Christo et Ecelesia." Our re- view of the series will appear in due course.

Literary Gleanings.

HALF-MAST HIGH. , One of the most universal of customs when a prominent man dies is the hoisting of flags on public buildings only part of the way up the flagpoles. This is known as " half-mast " ; but how many know what the custom means, or how it originated? To begin with, this practice was a military one. Ever since flags were used in .war it has been the custom to have the flag of the superior or conquering nation above that of the inferior or vanquished. When an army found itself hopelessly beaten it hauled its flag down far enough for the flag of the victors to be placed above it on the same pole. .This was a token, not only of submission, but of respect. In like manner, when a famous soldier died, flags were lowered, not only to indicate respect to his memory, but to show that he had suc- cumbed to the great conqueror of all--death, for whose flag space was metaphorically left at the top of the flag-staff.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
PAPER CANNON.

To the amazingly long list of articles made of paper there may be added paper cannon, which have been manufactured at the Krupp works in Germany. These paper field pieces are for special use of the infantry. Their calibre is a little less than two inches, and the pieces are so

light that a soldier can easily carry one. But the resistance is greater than that of a field piece of steel of the same ,calibre. It is not to be understOod that these paper guns are in any way to replace thOse made of steel. The paper arms are intended merely for uses in sitbations where the movement of field artillery would be impracticable. Paper artillery on the field .of battle seems an exti.aordinary thing; but it is hardly more so than paper wheels for freight cars on railways appeared when they were first introduced, or, for that matter, than paper water pails were.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VICTIM OF THE ZULUS.

In "Chapters of Reminiscence," by the late Dr. A. Taylor Innes, just published by Messrs. Hod- der and Stoughton, there are interesting recol- lections of his early career.

During a Ross-shire holiday he had the follow- ing experience: "Half-way bewteen his baptism of fire at Saarbruck and his burial of blood in South Africa, I saw the Prince Imperial in a distant region of Scotland. It was the evening when the Empress and her son, coming round from the Skye Railway, astonished the Inver- ness people by passing through their capital, and going on to Boat of Garten. Apparently she had been studying Bradshaw, and was struck with the romantic name of this remote Highland station, and resolved to spend the night there. There were several gentlemen in attendance upon her and. the Prince, and the dismay of the stationmaster when. the train having just roved away, they turned round and demanded the hotel, which was non- existent, and the sleeping accommodation which his house was too small to give,' was wonder- ful."

===== 0002601_19130823_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SOOTHING PIPE.

What is it about smoking that is so soothing to the nerves? The influence of the nicotine has very little to do with it, for no man enjoys smok-

ing unless he can see the smoke. It -Seems to be a psychological rather than a physical phe- nomenon. • PrOfeSsor B. S. Woodworth, of Columbia University, in his recently published book, "The Care of the Body," analyses it thus: "To see the light clouds floating. in • the air before one has a soothing influence. And as to the movements of smoking, Of What do they remind you?. Are. they not, in part, 'Very simi- lar to that earliest of movements exerted by the infant on his bottle, or on the 4 pacificator ' sometimes supplied in lieu Of a 'bottle? • There is -every reason why this particular 'movement should be soothing, and the alternate move- ment of blowing out the smoke is also of a gentle and quieting'sort, - The smoker has the advantage; in his moments of relaxation, of doing something easy and pleasing to himself. It is much like cracking .and eating nuts, or rocking in a rocking chair, or twirling one's moustache, and has the advantage of being more soothing than these other movements." Professor Woodworth insists that smoking is a distraction from work and should be "saved for moments of relaxation." With this, many

hard workers who work better when smoking will disagree.

BATTLES AMONGST INSECTS. . Insects and reptiles are now making their debut in the " movies" under - • Impresario Ditmars of the Bronx Zoo, New York City, says the "New York , World." A finish- fight by heavy-weight grasshoppers from the West Indies is one of the sporting novelties which Professor Ditmars projects upon the screen, in magnified . proportions . that make the hoppers look like gamecocks, to liven up his educational . •

illustrated lectures on natural histor3r. These creatures are active, powerful .in proportion to their size, and of exceedingly combative disposi- tion. Apparently they would rather fight • than eat up the crops _and vegetation. They have spiked legs, provided with spurs or gaffs, which they use as weapons against one another after .the manner of trained gamecocks. A favourite pastime of the Carib sports is to match a pair of these insect champions against one another in a regulation contest, which is fought on the fingers of the human umpire's hand— often to his serious discomfort. There is liberal betting on the favourites. In the same moral and instructive entertainment this wizard of the motion camera can show .you such un- accustomed wild life dramas as a Texas king snake exterminating the poisonous coral ditto; the horned toad of • Mexico lying in .wait in the sand, with-only his head sticking out, until an unsuspecting beetle saunters past, to be snapped up like a worm by the early.. bird; and the gay moth in its spectacular act of bursting full- fledged from the cocoon.

DECAY OF CLIMBING.

The sport of climbing began with Englishmen some fifty-five years ago. Its glory now has passed :to other lands. The original impulse of its founders has been, allowed to die. .In the old days, for every German who waS a profi- cient climber there Were twenty Englishmen. Now the exact converse is the case. •The cause of the decay of climbing is -obvious. Every sport which is v.orth the name requires an apprenticeship. In the case of football the public schools supply the necessary ,leaching and encouragement. Innumerable clubs pro- :vide a similar service in the case of cricket, golf, &c. There is no institution in England to foster climbing in our day. And the result is that the sport is stagnant in the country of its origin. -The Alpine Club, to which one would naturally look to perform this service to the nation, has fine traditions; highly honourable-in Levery way, but-it so-happens that this duty is not one of them. There were in the early days of climbing several reasons which might be said to justify this attitude of indifference. The original founders of the club were jealous for the quality of the sport. they had discovered. 'in these early pioneering days the fact of the seclusion and solitude and remoteness of the high Alrne rer,ions. upled with the sense of the spirit of discovery which insnired the climber. e,nstituted the most important element in the fascination of the sport.—" The Field."

THE WELSH EISTEDDFOD.

The Historical Side of an Interesting

===== 0002601_19130823_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
National Festival.

THE ANCIENT OFFICE OF THE BARD.

A mere Saxon will touch upon things Gaelic with very proper diffidence, and with many apologies in advance.- Especially so where the subject is so distinctly Welsh as the Eisteddfod. This much made clear, and the writer having demonstrated that he can spell the word Eisteddfod, a hint may be .given to ignorant Saxons upon the pronunciation., It is Ice-teth-vod, not Es-ted-fod. Carefully disclaiming any special knowledge of Welsh poetry (except such as may have been ruined by translation into English), we set forth here sonic historical gleanings upon the subject of Welsh Bards and the Welsh National Fes- tival. If they are fragmentary, it is because information exists only in Small and scattered fragments, and because its 'history (like Chat of the country itself) shows Many gaps and rents.

WHIT THE MODERN EISTEDDFOD IS.

The word Eisteddfod corresponds most closely to our word Conference. It ,means a session or 4 gathering for a special purpose, and is applied to the numberless local gatherings that mark the festival days in Wales, as well as to

the great annual gathering of the month of August. _

This Festival is held alternately in North and South Wales, and attracts a vast Multitude. National interest is fostered by the prizes for Literary efforts (poetry and prose), Fine Arts (painting, carving, 'sculpture), and Music (vocal, instrumental, composition). Classic works are rendered by an Eisteddfod choir, along with works composed lot. the occasion, and eminent singers give their best to a music-loving people.

_ Such is the modern Eisteddfod. It is not an Institution of learned men; it is a democratic gathering, which reflects most faithfully national taste and feeling, and to find anything quite like it we shall have to travel back to the vanished Greek' festivals of long, long ago.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
A RELIC OF A DIM PAST.

If the evidence were , but discoverable, it might, perhaps, be found that the Eisteddfod is an institution which the Gaelic tribes brought with them in their westward wandering. A thousand years of history may be fairly claimed for it. Tradition tells of an Eisteddfod under Taliesin in 517 A.D., and of another under Maelgwn, held on the banks of the Conway in 540 A.D. The assembly of 1070 A.D„ convened by Bleddyn, was marked by the issuing of new regulations for the Government of the Order of Bards, and a few years later we find Gruffydd, Prince of Gwynedd, who had spent his youth in Ireland (then famous as the land of the harp and song) reforming • Welsh minstrelsy and holding an Eisteddfod in 1100 A.D. in Caerwys in Flint, to which flocked "all the musicians of Wales, and some also from England and Scot- land."

===== 0002601_19130823_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE OFFICE OF THE BARD,

A word Ought, perhaps, to have been said at the beginning to explain that .the Bard from very early times held a fixed place in the life of the people. The . office of Bard was one. of national honour, and his place somewhat re- sembled that of our British Poet Laureate. In the courts Of the Welsh ,Prinees there was both a Bard of the Household and one .of higher rank, called the ""'cludred Bard. This high place was gained by contest, and wo given .to the poet-whoSe work was judged to be the best: His office involved not only the writing and singing of songs, but :,the telling of storieS for the entertainment of the Court, which stories would often, perhaps, be -told in verse, to, the twanging of the harp.

_ • The nationallbognition of poetry and song, and the constalit stimulus given by the con- tests, has prObably had much to do with the broad and beneficent interest in, music which has 'marked the Welsh people for so many .I_. . generations:

===== 0002601_19130823_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BARD OF THE HOUSEHOLD.

Of the office of the Bard of the Houseriold we know more than we know of tfie Bard who held the chair of honour. For there has-come down to us in the Statutes of Howel, a statement of his duties and 'privileges. We quote several from the thirteen regulations written concern- ing him :

The eighth court officer is the bard of the household.

He is to have his land free; and his horse in attendance, and his linen from the Queen, and his woollen clothing from the King. He is to sit at the three principal festivals neat the chief of the household, who is to place the harp in his hand. When a song is desired, the chaired bard is to begin; the first song to God, and the second of the King who shall own the palace; or, if there be none, let him sing of another king. After the chaired bard, the bard of the household is to sing three songs on various subjects. If the Queen desire a song, let the bard of the household go to sing to her without limi- tation in a low voice, so that the hall may not be disturbed by him. He is to have a cow or an ox from the booty obtained by the household from a border-country, after a third has gone to the King; and he is, when they share the spoil, to sing the "Monarchy of Britain to them. .He is to have a throw-board of the bone of a sea animal from the King, and a gold ring from the Queen. His penalty, if insulted, is six kine and six score of silver._ His worth is six score and six kine, to be augmented once.

This statement of Court etiquette as between the Chaired Bard and the Bard of the -House- hold suggests that the usage Was old even then, and the reference to the dividing of spoils taken during raids across the herder, to the singing of a song, gives a delightful sidelight upon the bad old clays of English and Welsh antagonism.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RISE OF THE EISTEDDFOD.

Coming back to the Eisteddfod and its his- tory, it becomes manifest at a very early period that the contest for the chair, which took place in the palace of every prince or ruler, was ele- vated to the level of a contest for a National "chair." Thus, in 1176 A.D. we have an account of how Bhys, Prince of South Wales, made a great feast at Christmas in his castle at Cardi- gan, and caused it to be proclaimed through Britain and IrelKid. The poets of Wales were summoned to a Man, and great was the contest. It is revealed in the record that there were two "chairs," one for poetry and the other for

LATIN AND THE ENGLISH TONGUE.

We welcome the proclamation .from our official Centre of Education that is to regulate ' the English tongue in its pronunciation of Latin. In some resrects Latin is', still a general language of Christendom, for the Boman Catho- lics have preserved it in speech. But while it has always remained the basis of education in our public schools, its use in 'speech hat fallen into dismemberment and decay. Many have tried to recall the accents in which Cicero spoke; but the only certain thing about it is that the English accent, as. generally used in public schools until a few years ago., would be unrecognised in the Elysian fields, no less than in any earthly field beyond this insular island. A language which is practically universal amongst 'Western scholars should' surely be intelligible in speech,- as well as in print. There-, fore that ,exclusively English rendering,of Latin words • should finally be abolished: It is ' absurd that a Fvnchtnan and an Englishman, quoting Virgil, sitoulcl not understand one an- Westminstei.
