THE PLAGUE OF MUSICAL ROGUES..

The Tudor period was a rosy one for the Bards. Both the Seventh and Eighth Henry gave Boyal Commissions for the holding of Eisteddfodau, and the Bardic offices, already greatly honoured, rose higher and higher in. general esteem. Then came the day of the vulgar imitator. Musical vagabonds invested the land. Loose livers twanged the harp and gained entrance into homes, for nefarious purposes, and the term Minstrel almost came to have the same meaning as thief. Laws 'were passed to .keep in check the "intolerable multitude " of musi- cians, and at the Eisteddfod of 1523 certificates were handed to the real Bards, which exempted 'them from the operation.of the laws thus made.. A very. interesting relic of the Tudor period is preserved in the Mostyn Hall library. It is a small silver harp, about six inches high, which went to the successful Bard at 'the assembly of 1568.

Another relic of • this. Eisteddfod, and quite valuable as showing how the event was then regarded, is the commission from Queen Eliza- beth, under which it was held. We quote a few paragraphs :

THE COMMISSION OF THE CAERWYS EISTEDDVOD, 1568. By the Quene. Elizabeth, by the Grace of God of Eng- land, ffrance and Ireland, Queue, die. To our trustie and right welbeloved Sr. Rich. Bulkley, Kt., Sr. Rees Gruffith, Kt., Ellice Price, Esq., one of our Counsaill in our Marches of Wales . . . Greating. Whereas it has come to our knowledge that vagraunt and idle persons naming theim- selves mynstrelles, Rithmers and Barthes, are lately growen into such an intollerable multitude within the principalitee of North Wales, that not only gentlemen and other by their shameles disorders are often,tymes dis- quieted in their habitacions. But also tkexpert mynstrelles and musicions in tongs therby much discouraged to travail in thexercise and pra,ctize of their knowledges, and also not a litle hyndered in their Lyvinges and -prefermentee. The refourma- eion wherof and the putting of those people in order the saide Lorde President and Conn- saill have thought verey necessarye and knowing you to be men both of wysdome and upright dealing, and also of Experience and good knowledg in the scyence have apounted and aucthorized you to he Commissyoners for that purpose. 0. The document recites that William Moslyn, Esq., and his ancestors have had " the gyfte and bestowing of the sylver harpe appertayning to the Cheff of that facultie," and goes on to say :

We require and command you by the I aucthoritee of these presentes not only to cause open proclamacions to be made in all ifayors, markettes, townes, and other places of assembly within our counties of Anglizey„ Carnarvon, Meryonneth, denbigh and filynt, that all and every person and persons that entend to maynteign their lyvings by name or Colour of mynstrelles, Rithmers, or Barthes . . shalbe and appears before you the said daye and place to show forth their learninges accordingly. But also that you . . . repayre to the said place the 'daye aforsaid, and calling to you such expert men in • the facultie of Welshe music as to you shall be thought con- venient to proceade to thexecution of the pre- misses and to admytt such and so many as by your wisdomes and knowledges you shall fynde worthy into and under the degrees. And geaving straight monycion and com- mandment in our name and on our behalf to the rest not worthy that they returne to some honest labour and due exercise, such as they be most apte unto for mayntenaunce of their lyvinges upon paine to be taken as sturdy and idle vagaboundes, and to be used according to the Lawes and Statues provided in that behalf . . . .

Yeven under our signet at our citie of Chester the xxiii of October the nynth years of our Raigne.

This effort to divide the sheep from the goats is very marked in this Commission. But that the evil .of vagabond minstrelsy was not easily suppressed is shown by another document, also kept at Alostyn Hall. This is, a petition from some Welsh gentlemen for an Eisteddfod in 1594. It complains to the Queen that the Bards of the "worthier sorte " are prevented in their calling by the crowds of " loYterers and droves," who infest the land. There is also in this document a list of the prizes which were granted. They are very curious and suggestive. For " poetrie " there Is the "silver chayre" ; for " harpeing," the silver harp; for " Crowthinge," the " sylver Crowth," and for the " atcane or singinge," the "silver tonge."

===== 0002601_19130823_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
DAYS OF NEGLECT.

With each decline of Welsh national feeling has come a decline in the time-honoured festi- val. This was most marked in the 18th century, when the Welsh national interest was at low- water mark. It need hardly surprise us that tile Eisteddfod declined, for had not religious feeling itself almost disappeared? Small won- der that music languished with it, .and that .even the harp and song left the people cold.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
REVIVAL.

Alas, for the debauched and careless 18th century.! But hail the 19th that brought re- vival! The Ersteddfod was warmed into life, and 1819 saw a great•gathering at Carmarthen, followed by others at Wrexham and Brecon. Steadily and surely interest has grown. The Eisteddfod of to-day attracts from ten to fifteen thousand .visitors, and all who know what song and music can do for We soul of a nation will desire that even this large number may be multiplied. • W. M.

A SUNDAY IN LUCERNE.

Switzerland the Country that Com- :Ales the Two Sublimates in Nature.

BY THE REV. E. MORGAN.

flichard Cobden on being. asked wtlether it was worth while to go a long journey to see Niagara, said "There are two subliznities in nature-7-one of rest, the other of. motion. The sublimity of rest is a distant view of the. Alps: The sublimity of motion is Niagara."

As a matter of fact, Switzerland combines the two forms of sublimity; it has many a huge cataract dashing over the steep cliff or precipice, as well as many a scene suggestive of repose. One of its charms is the wonderful variety of its scenery; now it is rugged, roman- tic, wild, then it is quiet, peaceful, and pas- toral. Lucerne combines both elements; it is indeed a beauty spot, and no wonder tourists from all parts of the globe flock to breathe its balmy and exhilarating air and to admire its enchanting landscapes. We are told that 200.000 travellers visit it each year.

Our party, consisting of seventy-two people (amongst whom were such well-known gentle- men as the Revs. Bamford Burrows and Samuel House), had gone on to Engelberg, under the genial escort of Mr. and Mrs. Anthony Barnes. Six of us decided to stay. for a day or so at Lucerne. Arriving early on Saturday morning, after a night of travelling, we were eager to survey the attractions of the place. On the morning of Sunday, June Bth, 1913, from the window of our temporary abode (Hotel Pilatus) the view was indeed sublime. Right before us was the placid lake of blue water; in the dis- tance towering mountains; some of which were covered with snow, and all bathed in the splen- dour of golden 'sunlight.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
ENGLISH CHURCH SERVICE ABROAD.

It is said that some friends away from home are not so careful to Observe the privilege of public worship; that they say they wish to enjoy the full benefit of the change and rest, and that they did not leave home and business to be imprisoned in It close, stuffy, and ill-ventil- ated atmOsphere. Fortunately none of us felt thus. Something is wrong when physical re- freshment is sought at the cost of spiritual loss. Finding that a service in the Church of Eng- land was to be conducted by Canon Ellison, we all went to hear him, and were not disap- pointed. He delivered a simple evangelical and eminently Protestant sermon. Outside that church, Roman Catholicism was rife and ram- pant and nine-tenths of the population belonged to that faith. The subject was "Eli trembling for' the ark of God" (1 Sam. iv. 13). He pictured the old man Eli, nearly 98. years of age, wait- ing for news from the battle, and showing greater concern about the fate of that which was a symbol of the Divine presence, than about his two,sons, Hophni and Phinehas. In the present state of the Christian Church there were features of encouragement, signs of pro- gress. The foundation was immutable, and the prospect was bright as the promise of God. Still there were circucmstances which were de- pressing. There is no room for despondency or panic, but great need for prayer and effort. A tender yearning solicitude for the mainten- ance and spread of the Gospel is an outstanding characteristic of the true child• of God. Sym- pathetically, the preacher referred to those mis- guided religionists who attached an excess of meaning to symbolism in worsTfip. It was hoped their faith did not rest in any inter- mediate object. No image can save, no relic has any efficacy. It was a delightful service • about six or-seven hundred people present, uniting heartily in the singing and in the responses of the liturgical part of the service. -All were by no means 'Anglicans, except in nationalities—many were Wesleyans, Quakers, Congregationalists, Bap- tists, but our differences were forgotten in the joy of common worship.

A FREE CHURCH SERMON.

Walking home after service along the Halden- strasse, a favourite promenade of visitors, we met a dear old friend from the homeland, who had worshipped in the same church, and to see whom was a very great delight, and when we returned to the Hotel Pilatus, we found the Bev. T. FeiTier Hulme, chairman of the Bristol district, actually waiting for our arrival. It was good of him, and we were pleased to be greeted by such a friendly face. When we found that he was going to conduct a service at the Polytechnic chalet that evening at eight o'clock, we made up our minds to go and hear and the service was a great ihspiration. It was held alfresco. The text was as follows: "Having loved His own which were in the world, He, loved them unto the end." There is no need to describe the discourse to anyone who knows the preacher. It was a, Gospel sermon, eloquently delivered. What struck us most ' was the number of references to names and in- cidents which were so familiar to us who be- longed to the same .Church as the preacher. Allusions to the Revs. W. 0. Simpson, McKenzie Cobban, and others, awakened pleasant memories and touched tender cribrds. Through the kindness of Mr. and Miss Hulme. we were able to return home in the Polytechnic's steam launch, and as we glided along in the darkness we saw the city beautifully illumined for the first time this season, and as we retired to rest, we thanked God for a Sunday in Lucerne. E. MORGAN. Epworth House, Padgate.

The following brief account of an interview with a St. Helens woman two years ago, and its sequel, will be read with keen interest by every citizen. NEARLY TWO YEARS AGO, Mrs. S. Jackson, of 52, Albert-street, close to the infants' School, St. Helens, said :—" Some years ago I was terribly bad with dropsy. The trouble came on just before a confinement and continued afterwards. It was all due to my kidneys being in such a disordered state. First of all I was taken with awful pains in the lower part of the back. I became nearly crippled with them, and many a time I have had great trouble to get out of bed for I could hardly straighten myself. The pains were at their worst first thing in the mornings. Then my limbs and body gradually filled with water, until I had become about twice my Usual size. "There was a scalding pain when passing the urinary excretions; they were discoloured, and contained a dark, heavy sediment. I got no better while I was having medical treatment, but one day I picked up a booklet, and read about a woman who had been cured by.Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, and began to use them. A TINY SOLDIER. A little china soldier On a little bracket stands, _ And he holds a little gun In his little china hands. But whether it is loaded I really cannot say, For he never pulls the trigger, And I hope he never may.

A BRAINY RACCOON.

Animals c-an use the power of reason. If you doubt it, what do you make of this? A party was travelling by stage-coach in America, and the coach stopped at a half-way house between towns.. A large raccoon wa., chained by the collar to a post in the frOnt yard After luncheon the driver of the stage gathered Up a plate of food from the table and carried it out to where the animal was chained. He sel the plate down just out of reach of the raccoon'., front paws: The animal strained and reached but could not cover the distance. One of the passengers said, "Why do you not place it within his reach?" "He can reach it," the driver replied. "He can't unless you move the plate nearer?' "Wait and \see," said the driver. The raccoon had by this time given up tryinr to reach the food, and sat whining. He seemed to ponder over the problem for a few moments then stretched his chain to its fullest extent turned round, reached out with his hind foot dragged the plate toward him. reversed the pro- cess, grasped it in his front paws, and proceed& to enjoy his dinner, much to the amusement o! the onlookers and the discomfiture of the man who was sure he could not accomplish the task

THE LITTLE PIANIST. One—two—three—four— Practising is sure a bore! How my little finger aches! Wish I didn't make mistakes! One—two—three—four— Practising is such a bore! One—two—three—four— Guess that's Bessie at the door; Yes, she's teasing Tommy's pup; Wish my quarter hour was up! One—two—three—four= Practising is such a bore! One—two—three—four— Oh, it rains. It's going to pour. And my kitty's out at play: I must fetch her right away! One—two—three—four— Practising is such a bore!

A GOOD METHOD. There-Was a little schoolma'am, Who had this curious way Of drilling in subtraction On every stormy day.

"Let's all subtract unpleasant things. Like doleful dumps and pain, And then," said she, " you'll gladly see That pleasant things remain."

A " MYSTERY " TRICK.

A trick that will cause a good (teal of amuse- ment and mystification an:ong young folks is played as 'follows:— A' small bare table is placed in the centre or the room, in sight of every.rie. Two ,siivei. coins—say, two shilling pieces—are placed on the table, some distance apart—one at the right hand, one at the left. A member of the company, who may Le cal:ed Sally, takes her place by the table, an another, whom we may call Jack, leaves the room. Sally places her hands, rr rathr.T her finger- tips, lightly upon the table. before the two ccins, cloSe to them', but not touching them, one to the right, one to the left. • "Now," says Sally, smiling at the company, "this is an experiment in telepathy. Iwill move one of these coins, and Jack will enter, and without word or sign, or so much as the quiver of an eyelash from me. will tell you which coin I moved. Which shall it be?" The company says, "The coin on your left." • Sally, with her left hand, takes the left-hand coin, raises it above her head, and waves it in a mysterious manner. "You observe," she says, "that. I make certain necessary passes." She then places the coin back on the table, just as it was, and her hand just as it was, and summons Jack into the room. lie enters, glandes carelessly at the coins, and without an instant's hesitation, says "Left." The trick is tried again and again. Neither Jack nor Sally can be detected in the least col- , lusion. Indeed, there is really no necessity for any of the usual signs; for, if those present are gifted with the power of keen observation, they will notice that the hand that. was raised above the head is, for the moment, very much whiter than the hand that remained 'on the table. If the reader will try standing beside a table, allowing one hand to rest upon it., while the other is, for a moment, held aloft., he will find that the test never fails; and yet it can be tried' for an hour, or an entire evening, without any- one detecting the trick.

Well Dear, what News?

"Nothing verk particular Mother; Baby's got a tooth, and Marjorie has bought anew hat--and, oh! she sends the recipe you asked for." "Oh yes dear, I quite forgot to tell you of the lovely Chocolate Cream and Trifle which Marjorie made when I was there last week." "Shall I read the recipe Mother? It seems awfully good, and so easy."

Marjorie's recipe to make Chocolate Cream: Take Milk. a pints, Sugar. I-5 Wove. and 1 packet (from a 4d. or ?id. box) of Chocolate Plavont BIRD'S BLANCMANGE POWDER.

/With 3 table-spoonfuls of milk well mix The powder in a basin into a !thin smooth paste. Boil the rest of the milk with Zion.. or 15 lumps of sugar. and whilst still thoroughly boiling pour all at mice into the basin, stirring well. When cold serve from a glass dish. For a delicious Chocolate Trifle take 4 sponge ,cakes, a little milk or sherry, some Ratafia biscuits and jam. Split the sponge-cakes. spreading with jam, and join again: cut in half longways and lay the pieces in a glass dish, • soaking with either milk or sherry. Prepare the Chocolate Cream with Bird's Blanc-mange Powder as above, and . when half cold pour very gently over the sponge cakes. .Garnish the top with some Ratafias or Glace Cherries, or crystalised fruit. Marjorie says:“ The Blanc-Mange MUST BE BYRD'S; there is nothing else so rich and creamy. There are 2 packets in the 4d. box, and 4in the 7id." BM 100

Pierced to the Heart.

When a wire broke the blocks should be knocked out of gear by the handle provided for the purpose. Taylor had been two years at the work and knew what he had to do in such a Case.

The Coroner: I suppose familiarity breeds contempt in this as well as in other things?— Yes.

Mr. Brothers: Do men attend to such cases? —Seldom, as the lads do it. Supposing men would put on a wire without stopping the machine, I suppose the lads would do the same?—Yes.

Have you ever had to speak to boys abou not stopping the machine?—l don't think have.

Dr. Anderson reported that he had conducted a post-mortem examination upon the body. The boy seemed to have been a healthy lad. He found a puncture in thp inter-costal muscles, between the third and fourth ribs, and on lift- ing the ribs he found that the heart was tended.

He described how he traced the small punc- ture caused by the wire into the right auricle of the heart, and that puncture, he said, caused the death of the lad. Had the wire end struck the rib instead of striking a -spot between the two ribs, the lad would not have been seriously Injured.

The Coroner remarked that such a sort of thing might not happen twice in a thousand years, and the doctor agreed that was so. "

The coroner said it was an accident that no forethought could guard against, nor could any one expect such a mishap. The jury, after a minutes consultation, re- turned a verdict of" Accidental death," and ex- pressed their sympathy with the relatives of the boy on the sad accident. Mr. Jenkins, on behalf of the firm, expressed similar sympathy. The jury added that they thought some notice should be placed about the machines, ordering them to be stopped when a wire broke or got entangled, and Mr. Jenkins said he would bring the suggestion before the firm.

STABBED IN THE HEART.

An Accident That Might Not Occur Twice in a Thousand Years.

A YOUTH MEETS STRANGE END. "This sort of thing might not happen twice in a thousand years," was the comment of Coroner Brighouse as to the remarkable cause which Jed to the death of Albert Taylor, 16 years, liv- ing at 34, General-street, killed in the works of Xlessrs.• Rylands Brothers at Church-street, Warrington, on Monday, 11th August. The inquest was held on Saturday. Mr. Brothers, factory inspector, attended, and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, for Messrs. Henry Greenall and Co., watched the case on behalf of Messrs. Rylands. John T: Taylor, the lad's father, said he heard on Monday night that his son had met with an accident, and when he arrived at the works Albert was dead. Henry Taylor (18), 12, Norman-street, said on Monday night, August lth, .he was employed minding four blocks underneath which the wire was wound on to bobbins. The deceased was attending four similar blocks alongside him. A wire sprang out of Albert Taylor's block and became wound round an axle on the shafting. Deceased seized the wire to get it off the, shaft, when it broke, and the end hit him in the chest. He did not speak, but staggered away, fell down, and was carried from the place. He then seemed dead. . Mr. Jenkins intervened with the remark that the axle, which the wire had got wound around, was going at the rate of 35 revolutions a minute. The Coroner: Can it be stopped? Mr. Jenkins: Yes, there is a handle which instantly throws it out, of gear. Evidently the boy, by pulling the wire, caused it to snap. Witness said when a wire got entangled in --the way mentioned they had orders to throw the machine out of gear at once. "Familiarity Breeds Contempt." John Langley, foreman fitter, said they had bobbins breaking occasionally, but not often.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS. Royalty's Patriotic Support of Home-made

Material.

AUTUMN AND WINTER FASHIONS.

The trousseau of the Duchess of Fife resem- bles, in its extreme simplicity and beauty, that of her cousin, the Duchess of Brunswick. The materials used are of the daintiest and finest, and the hand-work exquisite in its delicacy. The Princess Royal, in common with Queen Mary and Queen Alexandra, is patriotic in her choice of home-made materials; with the re- sult that the linen employed has been woven in Ireland, the silk fabrics in England 'and the tweeds in Scotland and Wales. Queen Alexandra takes the greatest interest in the preparation of the trousseau, and has given the Duchess much rare and beautiful lace- The bride has pleaded for an outfit of much smaller proportions than were considered fitting to the dignity of a Royal Princess in the days of old.

THE WINTER FASIIIONS. In the world of dress, time is taken by ,the forelock, and for some months past prepara- tions have been made for the autumn season-- now so near at hand; but the novelties and new designs will not be on view just yet. Still, it is possible to get some idea of coming modes, and glimpses of these reveal the fact that the rich brocades and damasks of a bygone time, which met with such a warm welcome when they appeared a few months ago, will,- together with velvet, be largely used for tailored suits of a very severe type, in subdued colourings, their adornment depending on the kind of but- ton used. So rich are these wonderful fabrics that they require no trimmings beyond the nar- nowest edging of fur. The revers on some of these suits are of the silkiest velvet, and so big, they have the effect of a shawl. Corduroy vel- vet is another material that will be much used this autumn, more especially in faded browns, mole and greens.

THE REVIVAL OF STITCHING. Covert coating, a most useful material, Is to be another favourite for tailored autumn suits, which are trimmed with a great deal of stitching. Coatings, serges, -and faced cloths, are never out of fashion, and will be just as much worn this autumn as in any previous year. All the skirts for the coming season are more or less open, and this gives width for walking, climbing, or for games. It is notice- able that in tire sports department, the Norfolk coat is less worn than heretofore, its place being taken by the straight-fronted coat, it will always find a place in sporting fashions, for it is thoroughly workmanlike and comfort- able. The new coats and skirts for the High- lands are all stitched at the edges in several lines. The leather, too, which for so many seasons bound the hem of the sportswomen's Raglan sleeves are seen on the skirts, has been replaced- by several rows of umber of sports coals stitch.greater i n g n , and these rarely have cuffs, and just a stitched strap and button, sufficient for ornament.

THE POPULARITY OF BUTTONS. Button manufacturers must rejoice at the de- mand that fashion makes for their goods. Of all trades that cater for masculine and feminine attire, they ought to be the • most prosperous just now. So great is the demand, that it largely exceeds the supply, and although work- ing at high pressure, orders cannot be executed immediately. Consequently the price of these indispensableadjucnecntt. sto dress h s has risen lastrisen fewqui m quite twoenntthys. -fl ve per Tailors are using horn buttons for the greater number of cloth suits for autumn wear.; Coats suitable for travelling, or to cover a dress of summer texture, are of Witney cloth, withi checked cloth lining, used also for collar, cuffs: and long revers. These have large bone bul4 tons, and are an indispensable addition to ai holiday outfit. Game-coloured tweed suits are this year made in most attractive shades and; mixtures, and nothing could be more comfort) able for country wear than these plain work- manlike coats and skirts, devoid of trimming, apart from buttons. SUMMER COATS.

Another summer coat for the holiday season may be made at tome ,of knitted fancy woal,i the fleecier the better. When' finished with i a roll collar of white fox, t is quite a luxurious garment. Numbers of new-woven effects have been "produced this summer for holiday wear; all are in the brightest colours to contrast with' the seaside white frock; delphinium blue, and buff are favourite tints, and for those who pre- fer more sombre shades there are beautiful tones of mulberry and brown, with tinges of yellow and gold. The popular shades for the silk-knitted coats with linings of cashmere— than which nothing could be cosier—are sky- blue, wheat and silver.

STOCKINGS AND RIBBONS. Silk stockings are.liils year made in many new designs, consisting of stripes and plaids, which are not always in the best of taste. The stocking that matches the tweed suit, however is to be recommended. It is woven to resemble a novel knitted stitch, and hartnonises per- fectly with the colourings of the Scotch tweeds,' with which it is worn. With respect to chine and brocaded ribbons, many are exact copies of old-world designs, of .at least a hnndred years ago. A hat is often entirely covered with them, but it seems a desecration to pleat them, or to make bows of them, as it is the fashion to do. The ribbon with the picot edge is also very attractive, and the raised velvet de- signs are most handsome. Coat collars are often covered at the back With ribbon to match, that on the hat. Sashes and belts are also made of ribbons, and a pretty idea for a Magyar blouse is ribbon cut into strips, two inches wide, and fastened together by a fancy stitch in silk, to match in colour.

PEARLS AS CORSAGE ADORNMENTS. There is no doubt that pearls are the favour- ite jewels of the majority of women. Many be- lieve that the ideal setting of pearls is the bare neck, consequently many corsages are cut to show necklaces and ropes of pearls vto the best advantage. Strings of various lengths are worn from the uncovered throat to far below the waist, in some instances falling in cascades over delicate and beautiful lace, which makes, next the skin, the best background for these exquis- ite jewels. Many of the ropes are imitations, but so marvellously real are they in appearance that even an expert may fail to distinguish them from genuine pearls, until submitted to a test. There is also a rope for large beads, as big as cherries, which are worn with the fashionable low-neck bodice. These are in the brightest colourings and made of 'wood.

MEDIC! COLLARS AND THE CARDINAL'S HAT.

The Medici collar has appeared in a new guise, for it is now made in all sorts of fabrics, and is expected to be a great success this autumn. In black velvet it is decidedly smart upon the littla coloured coatees, especially when the buttons on these are of xelvet to match. A great deal of attention has been given to obtain- ing the correct roll effect, which is more difficult to accomplish in fabric than it is in lace. A new-shaped hat has appeared in Paris. It is an exact imitation of a Cardinal's hat l and is made in soft velours cloth, of the colour worn by that dignitary of the church. It is decidedly striking in appearance, but becoming to youth- ful faces. The French sailor of fine straw, with a narrow brim and rather high flexible crown, will, be Much worn during the autumn. A band of soft ribbon is—at present—its sole trim- ming. Beaver is to be revived again this winter, in mole and dark, blue shades.

BIBLES IN DEMAND DURING WAR

Even during the most stressful period of the Balkan War, the Colporteurs of tife British and Foreign Bible Society report the sale of Bibles went on ceaselessly in the Balkan- States. In fact, the sale during 1912 was 3,000 copies more than in the .preceding peaceful Year.

THE BETRAYED SECRET.

How a Great Engineer's Plans Went Astray.

The bell of Boulton and Watt's great engi- neering factory at Soho, Birmingham, clanged the hour of dismissal on the eve of a winter's day' in 1780. There emerged, amongst the throng, two workmen, whose superior garb betokened that they occupied • better positions as servants of Tames Warr than most of .their fellows.

"Hello, Dick," sang out one; "be iliou going Up to the Wagon' the night?" "Aye, lad, I will be there. Thee wilt also?" replied Dick. "I am agoing!"

The men paSsed along their' separate ways. The " Wagon " referred to was an ancient hotel bearing the fuller title of "The Wagon and Horses," and was demolished in 1873. It was a favourite meeting-place of Watt's employes. An hour later, the men mei Tfl the low-roofed, oak-beamed parlour of tbe inn. One was Bill Smith, a foreman, and the other, Dick Cart- wright, a .pattern-maker, often introduced to the secret schemes of the great engineer. Joined by more of their fellow workers, it was decided to hold a concert, and Dick Cart- wright, ever a favourite, was voted chairman. The long churchwarden pipes were soon puffing merrily, and the, smoke mingled so pleasantly with the fumes of the freshly tapped ale that the company found itself in the best of humour.

Song followed song, glasses were replenished again and again, and the frolic was at its height when the door opened and in walked a stranger. He wore a thick overcoat, a shawl was tied loosely around his neck, his cap was pulled nearly over his eyes.. Soaked with the pitiless rain, falling without, the stranger drew a stool up to the blazing tire, and sat in moody silence as if ignorant of the merry company. His advent had a temporary depressing effect upon the other occupants of the room, but the arrival of more beer revived their spirits. They began to talk, not wisely, but too well. "Well, Dick,' said Tom Jones, a burly Welsh- man, "I hear you have been busy the day upon the new casts?"

"Oh, oh! I have," responded genial Dick Cartwright. , "I saw you were busy with Watt about them." - "1 was that, lad."

"He has got something particularly clever this time, I'll warrant, Dick? Don't you think so?"

"Oh, oh he's got something smart this time?'

The Stranger stealthily gazed at Dick, the last speaker.

"What be it now he's after?" queried the In- quisitive Jones.

"Well, something that is going to make a great improvement in his engines. The boss says it is going to revolutionise the whole of his business, and I can believe him."

More attentive did the stranger become. •"BEiin't Jimmy Watt a marvel," added Bill Smith. "'He's just that," chorused tfie rest of the com- pany.

"What is he going to do?" demanded another. "'Not much," answered Cartwright. "It is simple, but it is going to change altogether the action of the engines."

The man in the corner eagerly drank in every word spoken.

"But tell us what it niay be," asked another employe. '

"Not mach," tatalisingly rejoined Dick cart- wright,." but still it is a grand idea. Watt says the new method will give more .power to the engines 'with. the same amount of steam, and the boss says it will.make'the firm famous all over the, world. You know, however, he is .a Scotchman,•is what ,they. callatjour," and he won't say very much about.it. The stranger chuckled to himself. "But, Dick, you know, and you can tell us all about it.

"Yes, and Watt asked me to say nothing about it, because somebody else might get 'fold of the idea before he could have the patent rights taken out in London." "We shan't split."

Dick Cartwright, much against his conscience, was persuaded to sketch with chalk an out- line of the invention upon the table. All the company, including the stranger, gathered around.

Wonder was expressed at the ingenuty dis- played in the new machine.

"IL's simply great !" ejaculated Tom Jones, "Very complicated, though," said the stranger, speaking for the first time. "But, perhaps, it seems difficult to me, as I am neither an engineer nor a scholar."

Dick- good-naturedly repeated his explana- tion for the special behoof of the stranger, who, In turn, asked a good many questions. "I'm afraid," he said, "I'm too thick-headed to grasp the facts, but boys do not let this dis- turb you. Now, let us have a fresh round."

The 'beer brought in, the stranger proposed the health of James Watt, and added: "May he have all the luck he expects from his inven- tion."

The company enthusiastically drank to the 'toast, and shortly after the stranger departed. A.'few minutes later an alert businesslike per- son entered a hotel a short distance away, and d-emanded that a chaise be got. ready for a jour- ney to London. with the utmost despatch. ' The dispersal of a few guineas amongst the stable 'hands Promptly led to the fulfilment of his request. The stranger was soon speeding on his way with the unlucky James Watt's scheme fresh in mind.

A 'fortnight later James Watt entered the Patent Office to register his invention. "Too late, sir," was the ultimatum of the clerk In charge. "Patent rights for the same thing were taken out a week ago." Perplexed, vexed. angry, James Watt retired. Re never learned how he had been so roguishly forestalled.

DOG AND ITS DEAD MASTER.

Hearing a dog barking in the Thimber, the master of a keel looked towards the spot and discovered a powerful cross-bred Airedale and collie towing a man's body, to which it was attached by a leash round the wrist. The man proved to be Edward Burgoyne, 36, a Customs officer. At the inquest the coroner said either the dog or its master must have slipped over the quayside and dragged the other in. A ver- dict of Found drowned" was returned. "

What is reported to be the largest seal ever caught on the Cape Breton coast (Nova Scotia) has just been taken at Mabou Mines by a father and son. It weighed 8001 b. Theeskin measured ift. 10in. in length by 4ft. 4in. in width.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
LORDLY LIMEHOUSING.

Lord Wolmer Bitterly Attacks the Chancellor of the Exchequer.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
"POSITIVE EFFRONTERY."

By a curious coincidence, at the time the Labour leader, Mr. James Sexton, was proclaim- ing, on Saturday afternoon, at Bangor, the ex- cellent qualities of Mr. Lloyd George, Lord Wolmer, at the Town Hall, Newton-le-Willows, was denouncing most bitterly ,the Chancellor, and was seeking to inspire enthusiasm in his Tory audience by talking about the " effrontery " of Mr. Lloyd George. The political situation, said Lord Wolmer, had not altered very much during he last few weeks, except that Mr. Lloyd George had once again started his " Limehousing " campaign, which Made them feel as if they were back in the old times. When they heard those tirades against the idle rich and the landed classes 'being the oppressors of the poor they began to think they were back in the days before the great political scandal, and that they were being addressed by a statesman whose career had been absolutely unspotted in the past, and who could address his fellow-countrymen as a saint upon whose gar- ment of white there was no black mark, and who could reproach his fellow-countrymen on the way in which they used and obtained their wealth, as if every method he had employed to obtain 'wealth was _open to no criticism at all.

He thought it was positive effrontery for Mr. Lloyd George, coming as he did from the House of Commons, where he had to publicly apologise for his conduct and thus saved his resignation by the skin of his teeth, having been detected through no fault of his own—(laughter)—in transaction of which no Chancellor of the Ex- chequer had ever been guilty. Mr. Lloyd George accepted the tip from a Government contractor, out of which he might have made £30,000 if he .had not tried to make more, and held on too long. • "TO ROB THE CHURCH."

It was a serious matter when they had states- men in the position of Mr. Lloyd George, who, having escaped with the greatest possible diffi- culty the censure of his own party in the House of Commons, went about the country pretending that nothing at all had happened. It meant that Mr. Lloyd George did not realise in the slightest degree the gravity of his conduct in that busi- ness, or else it meant that he was deliberately trying to laugh it away. Mr. Lloyd George-was trying to set up a standard for his political opponents which he had never attempted to attain himself. When they remembered that this statesman was attempting to use hig power in order to dis- member and to rob the Church of England, then they could see no greater outrage committed against political decency in our time. The more they thought of Welsh Disestablislimmt the more were they struck by its incomparaille meanness and the absolute wickedness of the measure. He thought the consciences of the neople in this country were being roused. as they had never before been roused. In that robbery from the Welsh Chureh Mr. Lloyd George and the Liberal party were kring assisted by the so-called Independent Labour party. An instance of their so-called independ- env) occurred during the Committee stage if the National Insurance Art Amendment Bill, which was s, subject of vital impnrtance, not only to the Labour party, but to all trade unionists in the country.

LABOUR PARTY ATTACKED. Under that Bill four Commissioners had been established in England, Wales, Scotland, and Ireland—a _sort of preparatory Rome Rule to please the little sections of the coalition. They could readily understand the great trade union organisations and friendly societies finding it difftcult and .inconvenient to work under four different sets. of Commissioners. The Labour party, egged on by the trade unionists in the country, proposed an amendment that there should be only one Commissioner throughout the whole of the United Kingdom. The Unionist party cordially supported that amendment, but in the middle of the debate the Labour party began to realise that they might perhaps carry through their amendment, and that possibly the Government might be defeated. With one accord they turned tail and ran away from their own amendment, and the only people to vote for it were the Unionist party. It was simply because the Labour party had started on one of their make-believe fights to show their independence, but when they found -there was just a chance of carrying it against the Govern- ment they abandoned it helter-skelter and got out of it the best way they could. What a pitiable state of sycophancy that revealed

===== 0002601_19130823_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
TEE TWIN'S RECORD

Kentish twins have gained the prize for regu- lar attendance at school. For nine years they have not missed roll-call. Year after year they have trudged solemnly and persistently to school. In all those years they have never played truant. One seems almost obliged to compliment those twins, -says the " Daily Chronicle," on their abstinence from truancy, the splendid search for education with the teacher at the end of it; the official teacher who marks down the attendance form.

Clocks are now made to run five years with once winding up. In 1881 the Belgian Govern- ment placed one of these in a railway, station and sealed it. It has kept capital time, having c only been four times wound—in 1886, 1891, 1896 an 1901; and there is a clock' in the Church of St. Quentin, in lifayence, which has only stopped once during a period of 500 years.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0224.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
. • -

===== 0002601_19130823_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
'TIE GREATEST FIGHTER." Labour Mai- Pays a Tribute to . Mr. Lloyd George.

BITTER STRIKE RECALLED

The greatest fighter that the world has ever produced was the high tribute paid by Mr. James Sexton, of the National Union of Dockers, and prospective Labour candidate for St. Helens, to Mr. Lloyd George at a meeting, to organise_ the workers, held at Bangor on Satur- day.

Mr. Sexton regretted that he could not address his auditors in Welsh. -(Laughter.) There had been a time, he said, when he could have addressed them in Irish, but his associa- tion with the "brutal and bloody Saxon" had somewhat eliminated his acquaintance with the Irish 'language. Although he was not born in Ireland, he was born and bred in an Irish atmos- phere, and a very rebellious atmosphere, too. He was born a rebel against the then existing state of society, and Tie was still a rebel in some respects against the now existing state of society. If he was anything, he was a Consti- tutionalist.

This reference, of course, to the "bloody and brutal Saxon" was a figurative one, and a quotation which, he hoped, would not be taken offensively even by the Saxon. (Laughter and applause.) His association with Wales had chiefly been with the men of South Wales, but there was one occasion when he came to North Wales to co-operate with the representative of that city, Mr. Lloyd George. (Cheers.) That was during the existence of the Penrhyn quarry dispute. Mr. Lloyd George was not then the Chancellor of the Exchequer, and did not, prob- ably, anticipate that he ever would be,Chancel- lor of the Exchequer.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
"AN EYE-OPENER."

Mr. Sexton said that he and Mr. Lloyd George acted together on a committee to en- deavopr to settle the Penrhyn dispute. With- out any political intent, he would say that while North Wales produced such men as 'Mr. Lloyd George there would never be any fear of Wales losing its language or its nationality. (Cheers.)

First -and always he (Mr. Sexton) was a Labour man, and when in co-operation with their member he came to assist in settling the Penrhyn dispute, he got an insight into the con- ditions of land tenure in Wales which was an eye-opener even to him, who knew ,a good deal about the robbery which had taken place of the people's land all over the country. Ile was not going to talk politics, but he could not forego • the opportunity in Mr. Lloyd George's constituency of paying what he considered a just tribute to the greatest fighter that the world and not North Wales only had over produced. (Cheers.) Mr. Sexton proceeded to state the case for the organisation of labour, and urged all workers connected with the transport trades to join the Dockers' Union, which was already 50,000 strong, and was affiliated with the National Transport Workers' Federation, com- prising some 300,000 men.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
"CATCH MY PAL."

Mr. J. A. Seddon, Labour ex-M.P., and ;pros- pective candidate for the Newton Division, also spoke. Science applied to industry, he said, was vastly increasing the wealth of the world, but from the workers' point of view the effect was likely to he disastrous. It was making a few people rich beyond the dreams of avarice, but, on the other hand, was condemning the great mass of, the population to be haunted with a lifelong fear of poverty. The workers were doing no good by making millionaires. If the millionaires were nice-looking, he would not object so much, but most of them were as Ugly as sin. (Laughter.) In a good many working men the old slave spirit still survived, and they should prove that they were in deed as well as name free mend :This they could do, and one of the means of doing- it was trade unionism. He hoped all the Workers present would join the Dockers' Union and adopt the, policy of 'Catch my pat" until there was not a man of their acquaintance who was outside that beneficent organisation. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19130823_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

INJURED BY RUNAWAY MOTOR

Mrs. Florence White, 9, Stonehill-lane, Run- corn, was injured, through being knocked down by a runaway motor cycle in Lime-street, Liver- pool, on Monday. She was detained at the hospital, suffering from shock, an injured leg, and internal injuries.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
UNBEATEN AT WATER POLO

Widnes, water polo team met Garston at the latter's baths on Monday night, and made a draw of three goaia. Widnes are now the only unbeaten team in the, Liverpool Polo League.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
PLUMS FIRST; BIRCH AFTER.

Two Runcorn boys, Walter. Dean (12) and Arthur Davies (8), have discovered that after sweet comes bitter. They stole 111 b. of plums from the market and on Monday they received, by the order of the magistrates, four, strokes with the birch each for the theft. •

===== 0002601_19130823_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ART OF BREAD-MAKING.

--- The art of bread-making, said Mrs Harry Baker, of Runcorn, opening the Weston Flower Show, near Runcorn, on Saturday, was a forgot- ten industry amongst present-day women; some even had forgotten the art of cooking. It ought to be essential for a young man, before marrying, to see that the young woman in the first place should be able to make and bake bread. They were relying nowadays upon too ra-tfch tinned stuffs. Every household larder ought to be provided with stocks to last at least for three months in case of an emergency. The principal prize-winners were: E. A. Cald- well, E. A. Jones, W. Richardson, J. Bell, J. Hayes, William Ham, J. W. Moore, J. T. Harri- son, T. Robinson, F. Norton, A. Talbot, J. Brown, L. Naucollis, Grange Moore, J. W. Jones, T. Tomlinson, G. H. Rathbone, W. H. Banks, D. Orme, and J. E. C. Banks.

THE CYLLISI"S NOTE BOOK Theßival Claims of Wales and the Lake District.

BY " SILVER STREET."

CHESHIRE REST HOUSES-VIII.

Cornhill Farul, Dunham Hill, near Chester. For those Warrington riders who wish to stop short of Chester, this good old farmhouse is placed in exactly the right spot. Situated on the roadside, 14i miles from Warrington Bridge, it is a very pleasant afternoon's ride to Corn- hill Farm, and there is no need to come the same way back, for the by-roads on the left lead through Manley to Delamere Forest, by Bangers Bank, one of the finest and safest coasts in the county, or by a Mlle further round through. Ashton, Kelsall and Delamere village, to the, well-known toll bar at Oakmere corner.

Mrs. Blackburne'not only caters for the after- noon visitor, but can accommodate those who wish to spend ,a longer holiday in this charm- ing valley of the Gowy. Being only five- miles from. the Cathedral city, is a point in itself ,and then the villages of Trafford, Manley, Alvanley, Ince and Barrow are within walking distance. But the gem is Plemstall Church, first founded in the times of, Alfred by a good man, who became one of the first archbishops of Canter- bury. No Vv'arringtonian should be ignorant of this fine old church, and yet how many dash on to Chester', when a detour of a mile would give them a Scene 'of such interest! To return, to Cornhill 'arm, where we started, everything' is kept there, from pigs to ferrets, from cows to turkeys, and there is a large garden thrown in, from which you can watch the continuous stream of passing vehicles on the road to and from the county town. OAKMERE CORNER.

Talking about a stream of vehicles, I believe it is a fact that the scouts stationed near the toll bar at the cross-roads-in the middle of, the Forest registered a total of between 2,000 and 3,000 motor cars and autocycles passing that point on Bank lioliday, from 10 a.m. to 6 p.m. I was asked also to guess how many times the toll-house had been charged by these vehicles during, the last seven years, and said I should think things would be warm enough at seven times a year. Perhaps my readers will be as astonished as I was when I was told the acci- dents of that kind have numbered 130 during the period mentioned. Now, if only there was a pit there, instead of a house!

===== 0002601_19130823_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
TO THE LAKES.

!was asked lately by a correspondent whether. a three days' ride could-T.a. better spent in Wales or the Lake District, starting, of course, frorn.Warringtoh.. In Wales decidedly, to those who are strange to either. To get to the Lakes we must cross our great colliery district and go. through Preston, and so the fare is well saved by cycling to Lowton and taking the train to Preston. By doing so the fare for the cycle comes within the sixpenny limit, and the road north of Preston is excellent, and through -ex- cellent country. Lancaster is itself full of in- terest, and the Lake District commences soon after Carnforth is passed. But to those who wish to cycle all the way, Elie Lakes are " off " for, a week-end holiday. One would be spent in getting there and another in coming .back, and 'there is no alternative route, .since the Irish. Sea blocks it on one side and the Pennines on the other. Better_ f7ir. take the train to Win- dermere Station, or t6-Griinge, and make either of these towns your starting-point, and I will give the 'best, and Most practical ways. Of -seeing. the Crikes next week. But remember • that August and September are among the wettest months up there.. The driest (or should we say the least .wet)- is, strange to 'say. April, and therefore an Easter or Whit week holiday is [he most enjoyable in England's playground. And the motor char-a-bancs have not then started running. . ,

Lamp time to-day 8.24. Next Saturday 8.8. The nights are drawing in more quickly at this time than any other during the half-year.

BOWLING.

The Coming Match in Aid of Local

===== 0002601_19130823_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
Ch4rities.

THE MAYOR AS A BOWLER.

The bowlers will be busy to-day skimming the jack on the Horse and Jockey green, in a match to aid local charipes. A long entry has been secured, and some good play should ensue.

There is a probability that Major G. It. Cros- field, president of the Works Association will lead off with the Mayor, who is figuring in the Caledonian Association team, as his opponent. Some of the more noted local players are taking part.

The match will undoubtedly prove interestirg, and that alone ought to attract a large "gate," while an additional incentive for the public to attend in large numbers should be the fact that by their presence they will be helping the chari- ties of the borough.

Owing to this match, none of the fixtures in the handicap will be played. These will be postponed until to-day week, August 30th, and upon that occasion the Secretary would like the players to take the green prompt on time.

The draw for the Works Association annual handicap gives promise of some first-class play. The entry is large, and the interest should start with the first round, to be played a week to-day, and be continued right to the final. In the first bout some well-matched players meet, and they will doubtless afford interesting exhibi- tions.

The final for the "Crosfield " Cup is to be played to-day, on the green of the Red Lion Hotel, Prestwich, and an exciting game is anti- cipated. Rylands' meet the Wigan Subscrin- tion. Will Rylands' add another tronhy to their list this season? I hope to see them do so.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
INDIVIDUAL MERIT CHAMPIONSHIP

It was resolved at the Council meeting of the Warrington and District Amateur Bowling Association this week that the Individual Merit Championship commence on August 30, instead of August 23rd. in view of the final of the association, which takes place on Saturday, August 23.

This is as it should be. It would hardly have been fair to all concerned to run two imnorte.nt events on one day. The entrances close for the Individual Merit on Wednesday, August 27th.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
CEMETERY v. CONSERVATIVES

The match to-day between the Cemetery Hotel and Senior Conservatives at the Cemetery, is going to attract a great many bowlers, and will certainly be worth, watching. The Crosfield " Cup will be presented to the winners after the games terminate.

ANGLING. Warrington Workingmen's

Association.

N.A. CENTRE

L tRGE ROACH.

Although sport has not been plentiful this past fortnight, some very fair specimens have been recorded. Roach from the Gowy have been of a good size, and have given anglers plenty of sport. For three weeks in succession roach well over lib. have been recorded at headqikarters, but they .are well behind, as we have specimen roach from the Gowy 11b. 50z. Bde. Still, the 11b. -roach is not common, and the anglers who can take fish of this class must congratulate themselves. AN ANGLERS PARADISE.

We have reports from Messrs.. H. Moore and E. Hill, of their .catch at the Broads, Great Yar- mouth. Their heaviest roach Were 11b.. 7oz. I,sdr. and 11b. 6oz. 7dr. ; bream, 21b. lloz. 101 b. The sport they had was grand, and Mr. _C. 'Ashton will come in for many. congratulations for introducing such a water to club members. Messrs. Moore and Hill say. it is "an anglers' paradise." During their worst day their catch was well over 201 b., and during their best day over 601 b. of fish were taken., They report,that you do not want any.great quantity to make up 601 b., when you are getting roach up to 141 b. and bream 2,11 b. Their catch on the last day was the best. The roach they brought home for their Warrington friends. There must have been between 401 b. and 501 b. of roach, and every one was from 11b. to 11b. 70z. 15dr. Several London and Sheffield anglers were fishing in the same stream, Mit they had not the success that ow locals had, and they were surprised to see our lads turning back 11b. roach and bream up to 21b. When they were not taking anything ,over the Ilb., Of course, they wanted to know the reason, and Made inquiries from them, and many were helped over the stile, and put. on the right lines to fish for specimens. Many friends were made, and one angler from down South has invited them to have a try in the Thames. They have accepted the invitation. .and probably we • shall be recording weights from the Thames berore next season is out.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
CANALS OUT OF FORM.

The canals .are quite out of form, vith the exception of Chester. Again the length in the town is turning out the best. • Norton this last fortnight- is entirely off colour. The Gowy:is providing better sport than was anticipated, and several, nice bags. have. I.Ven caught. There is more water in than was ex- pected. Several holes Stnier way could not be approached during .the week, but- report's stor, it is very clear, and anglers must approach tfie stream very carefully to meet with success. BILLIARD LEAGUE

Enthusiastic Meeting the Forerunner of a

Successful Season.

SECOND DIVISION FORMED.

What the Chairman "(Mr. It C.ollin) declared to be the best-attended meeting ever held under the auspices of the Warrington Billiard League, to place at Bylands' liecreation Club on Muilday evening to elect officers and make angements for the ensuing season.

CLUBS AND RULES. The decision of the cOmmittee to form a second division, to cope with the growing popu- larity of the game in town, was very well re- ceived, and the following entries were made :

FinsT DIVISION. SECOND DIVISION Conservative Club :onservative Club.

uthor 'Cons: Club,

Emmanuel Church. Crnstield's. funior Liberal Club. Our. Lady's

P. Walker and Son. Lea ;le of the Cross Parish Church.. ?St Mary's B.C. Latchford Cons. Club. ?St George's. Liberal Club. .

It will be seen that the first division will con- sist of ten clubs, instead of twelve as last season, and that the second division consists of 'seven clubs, with the possible addition of St. Mary's and .St. George's, against whose names are placed query marks, as :they asked for a little longer time to giye definite answers. The next meeting was fixed for September 9th, at the Conservative Club, when the list will be finally made up. • Among the decisions arrived at, upon the recommendations made by a special committee that had revised the rules, were the following:. Entries _close September. 6th, by which time a list of registered players must have beeh fur- nished to the secretaries..

Number of players to be registered by each club: In the first division, 16; .second divi- sion, 12. . .

Number of players in, competing teams:— First division, 8; second division, 6.

Season for' playing matches to begin the ,hird week in October:

Matches may. begin early in the evening, but not later than 8 p.m. There is to be no longer interval than fifteen minutes between games, players failing to appear to proceed with play within this speci- fied time, to concede full points to their oppo- nents.

No game is to begin after 10.0, except by mutual consent. Unless this mutual arrange- ment is made the games will be carried forward to .another evening. AN ENCOURAGING REPORT.

, Mr. JOHN CRAIK (joint secretary with Mr. J. Cliffe), in presenting the secretaries' annual report, said the hopes of those who founded the league had been fully ,realised. Interest in billiards in town, especially among the smaller clubs, had increased by leaps and bounds, while the bringing tOgether of members had promoted the growth of friendship in a manner that was contemplated would be the case. Among the notable performances in the league was the way in which the Senior Conservative , club (winners) went down to Walker's to be ' beaten. (Laughter.) That, of course, gave Walker's Club a great fillip, and caused them to be talked about in the way they desired. (Ap- plause.) Ilylands' went down to Our Lady's, and found them a little too much for them. (Laughter.) These events showed what could be done if the players put their hearts into the game. Walker's Club kindly provided the extra accommodation for the match ,which de- cided who should hold the cup, and the Con- servatives won. Then they had the pleasure of seeing the winners of the trophy play the " talent " of the League in a match that was full of interest; and, of course, the " talent " was no good, or was, for the moment, not up to the mark, , tlioughtbr.) They were desirous, as a league, and as individual clubs, to give all the Ihelp possible, and it had been noticeable that the smaller clubs had been anxious to show their appreciation of. the attention that had beeh Shown them by the larger clubs. • (Hear, hdiif.) They Were all convinced that the league had ij bright futute in prospeet.. (Hear, hear.) Fa ttiOSe chibS With small tables only, he hoped to subithi the details of a scheme for a league .of tileit,6iiin. (Applause.) Islf. Cfaik Was complimented upon the elo- ' 060 Manner in which he had presented the admirable rePOrt of the joint secretaries, which was unanimously adopted. ELECTIONS.

On the motion of Mr. J. Naar, seconded by Mr. DAY, Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., was re-elected president. Mr. Hy. Collin (Conservative Club) was, on the motion of Mr. W. 131asy (Emmanuel), sec- onded by Mr. PERRY (Whitecross), re-elected chair Man ; and Mr. Mullery (Liberal Club) was re-elected vice-chairman.

Messrs. Craik and Cliffe were re-elected joint secretaries, Mr. Cliffe consenting to serve an- other year under pressure, having intimated a desire to resign.

Mr. H. Maddock was, on the proposition of Mr. HORRABIN, re-elected treasurer. Messrs. Williams and Russell were re-elected auditors.

FOOTBALL.

The Warrington Team Moves. 'RACTICE MATCH ATTENDED BY OVER 1,000 SPECTATORS.

A Start in Earnest.

Last -Saturday, under a burning August sun, the players of the Warrington Club took part in a practice match between picked sides. All the members of the yam that so nearly won the Northern Union Cup last year are available for the coining SQ/ISOII, and although the Welsh players, Nicholas and Cox, \-Vho have. been home for the summer, were not due in War- rington_ till to-day, there was a good muster, and 'a fine game was witnessed by a thousand spectators, who paid one penny on the popular side and twopence on the reserved side for the privilege of admittance. The "gate" was about £5 or £6, and goes to charity. These. practice matches are to be continued until the opening of the season, when, it is hoped, the players will be in such good form that they will imme- diately begin to gather points. Leigh opens the season for Warrington at-home.

It will be highly satisfactory to the members of the club, and the supporters generally, to know that an exceptionally fine list of home matches has been arranged for the holidays, upon which Mr. T. P. BradshaW is to be con- gratulated. •• Holiday matches are great att,rac- tions, and upon these special occasions the like- lihood of winning is greater at home than away.

The team will be under the care of Ish Hackett, once more, and past experience teaches us that that is the best possible arange- ment. The ground is in splendid condition, and. great. activity prevails in official quarters, with a view to having, 6verything in apple-pie order by-September 6th. We shall miss Mr. H. Ashton and Mr. T. Davies from places they have occupied with honour and distinction for so long; but Mr. T. P. Bradshaw, and, those with whom he is working will, we know, step into the breach to some purpose.

SO far, only two new players have been secured—Buddick and Davis, of Coventry—who did not turn out last Saturday. Renwick was also among the absentees.

ASSOCIATION. Warrington and District League.

A successful meeting of the Warrington and District Association Football League was held ' on Wednesday evening at the Lion Hotel. Mr. Wright occupied the chair, owing to the absence of Mr. P. Booth through indisposition. The Secretary reported having received the rules of the League back from the Liverpool County Association Executive Committee. They had been considered very satisfactory, with two exceptions, and these were now confirmed by the League. Applications for membership to the First Division were read from Orford Barracks F.C. and the Wirral Refining Co. F.C., and wete unanimously accepteth .

Sir Gilbert Greenall wrote -consenting to become president of the League. - There are vacancies for two or three more clubs in both Divisions, the First with a radius of ten miles and the Second a radius of six miles from Nfarliet Gate, Warrington. Mr. R. Brandreth, Orford, Warrington, is the secre- tary.

THE BRIDE AND BRIDEGROOM.

The .Padgate Wesleyan Church was the scene on Thursday of the marriage of Miss Ethel Mason, younger daughter of Mr. James Mason, of Fearnhead House, Fearnhead, and the Rev. William Baby, the elder son of Mr. William Baby, of Anligld Plain, Durham. The church was tastefully. decorated by friends of the bride with white flowers and foliage, and the service was conducted by the Bev. E. Mor- gan, assisted .by the Bev. W. A. Beckett. . . . •

- The bride, who was escorted up the aisle and given awforiby her father, was much admired in a simple, but,effective, gown of white embroid- er9d voile, with a veil of Brussels net and wreath of orange blossom. She carried a shower bouquet of white roses, lilies and heather.

There 'were 'three bridesmaids—Miss Mason (sister of the :bride), Miss Annie Baby (sister of the bridegroom) and Miss Gwen Bennett (daugh- ter of Dr. !Bennett). They wore gowns of floral voile and hats of black tufle, trimmed with pink roses. Their bouquets were composed of rambler roses, gathered from the garden of the bride's home.

Mr. James Baby (brother of the bridegroom) acted in the capacity of best man, and Mr. J. Herbert Mason (brother of the bride) as grooms- man. Mr. George W. Weaver presided at the organ, and the ushers were Mr. Gordon Burgess and Mr. Hugh Mellor. The children of the Padgate Wesleyan Sunday School primary department strewed flowers as the happy couple .left the church. The ceremony was attended by Many friends, and afterwards a reception was held at Fearn- head House, the grounds of which were appro- priately decked with flags and bunting, through the kindness of Mr. George Mather. The, bride's travelling costume was navy blue, with a smart hat to match, and the honeymoon is being spent in North Wales. . The number of presents exceeded. one hundred, and included one from the primary department of the Sunday School and another from the Junior Wesley Guild.

The Rev. and Mrs. Baby are eventually to take up their residence at Aspatria, Cumber- land.

WORKS CLOSED.

Messrs. Robertson, Morris Brook, Shut Down in Consequence of a Strike.

THE ATTITUDE OF THE MEN.

Some 48 members of the Gasworkers' and General Labourers' Union in Warrington have been on strike this week, in consequence of a dispute and alleged -grievances at the wire works of Messrs. Robertson, Ltd., Morris Brook, Latchford. A fresh development occurnal east night, when the fthil .notified all hands that

they Were reluctantly: compelled to close the works. They returned all the men's insurance cards and paid up all outstanding wages.

The trouble at the wireworks has been of nearly two months',standfng.. Some time ago there was a conference upon, the overtime ques- tion. The men asked for overtime rates at time and a quarter for the first two hours and then at the rate of tilife and 'a half. The firm desired the 'men to complete a 54-hour Week before overtime was paid.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0279.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STRIKE COMMENCE!'z.

The men came out on strike on TuestLiy. "Shortly after the strike began," said Mr. I:. Dukes, to an "Examiner ", representative last night, ." Messrs. Robertson sent a message stating that Mr. Robertson WaS pre- pared to address the: men 09 strike inside the works. The metkreplied .that they were pre- pared to attotid Such a meeting, provided a representative of their union accompanied them. This -was refused and the men remained out.

"A notice was posted at the work's yesterday," :ontinued Mr. Dukes, "stating that owing to an nsufliciencrof men the firth reluctantly decided o stop the works. The insurance cards WCre .eturned and the men paid last night. In all, 'bout 70 men are affected. '

"Our view is that the. matter hangs upon he question of reducing, a member of the mion's wages by 2s. a week, and we think .t) ew minutes' conference would settle the wholc hing.

"We are prepared to meet the firm, with the view of arriving at an amicable settlement, but up till now the opportunity has not been given."

Doctors' coachmen in Berlin wear white hats. This enables the public to promptly recognise a, physician's vehicle, in case his services are sud- denly required.,

===== 0002601_19130823_art0280.txt | included=true | included =====
TRUTH WILL OUT.

The Tariff Beforin League Handbook, on page 286, under the heading "The Invasion of the Colonies," sets out the exports of the United States to Canada and other British Dominions, in order to prove that " protected " America is beating the Free Trade Mother Country out of "its own markets." It is amusing, therefore, to find in the ,League's ,".Monthly Notes" for August the following candid admission. It is not, indeed, news to the Free Trader; but it shows the League in the interesting attitude of eating up its, own illegitimate "poisit ":

"It is quite, true that our proportion of Canada's total imports has fallen from 24.9 per cent. in 1901-2 to 20.3 per cent. in 1912-13, while that of the I:Ailed. States has risen frcim 58.4 per cent. to 65.5 per cent. It is quite natraiil,' and, indeed, inevitable, that such should be The cage. As Mr. Wickes points out :

"The figures for the United States include items to a very considerable value Which are non--competitive in every sense of the term so far as British trade with the Dominion -is concerned. I refer to items, e.g., such as raw cotton, kerosene and petroleum oils, fresh fruits, agricultural products generally, ccral,:and lumber, and manufactures of wood, such as waggons, carts, buggies, sleighs, &c.'

"Moreover, the United States is Canada's nearest neighbour, and therefore possesses all the ,advantages arising from such a position. A Canadian buyer can communicate r with an American manufacturer by telephone,, and can obtain much speedier delivery than if the goods were ordered from this country. Fashions and styles which have been accepted in the States are, in many cases', 'adopted in the Dominion. Many Canadian business men have received their training in the United States. The cost of freight and packing falls much lighter on American trade than on British, owing to the proximity' and easiness of access between the two countries. With such advantages, it would be strange indeed if American trade with Canada did not .out-distance British trade."

The cost of the Navies of the world last year aggregated £145,000,000. There is 281 b. of blood in the body of an aver- age grown-up person.

Members of Parliament in Belgium are paid £l7 per month whilst Parliament is sitting. It costs the London County Council £6,000,000 to educate the 660,000 children in the schools controlled by them.

TERRITORIALS IN CAMP.

Warrington Men Having a Merry Time

In Wales.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0289.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK COMBINED WITH PLAY.

FROM OUR SPECIAL CORRESPONDENT.

The Warrington Territorials are having. a merry time under canvas at this attractive Welsh town, and they are profitably combining worii w.i pleasure. We are located in the pic- turesque Vale. of Clwyd, and the camp is situ- ated in a very pretty spot. It would be diffi- cult to find a more ideal pitch or a better camp- mg ground than that on which we are now encamped. The splendid scenery around, and the good state of the ground 'combine to make things very comfortable for the troops. The battalion forms part of the South Lanca- shire Brigade, consisting of the 4th South Lancashires and the 9th and 10th (Scottish) Liverpools. The Brigadier is Colonel Macfle, and Captain Bellamy is acting as Brigade- Major. The Warrington lads are under the able command of Lieut.-Colonel B. Fairclough, and the other officers in camp are: Majors F. I. Appleton and G. R. Crosfield ; Captains C. F. Cary, A. B. Layton, H. G. Roberts, H. Owen, F. M. Monks and E. Fairclough ; Lieutenants L. S. Henshall, M. Woods, 0. L. Whittle, E. Fair- dough, Skinner and McClure; Second-Lieuten- ants C. N. Roberts, R. A. Seymour-Jones, E. L. Frost and B. T. Dickson; Lieutenant G. H. Ingram (quartermaster); Surgeon-Major J. Adams and Captain E. L. Elgood (adjutant).

===== 0002601_19130823_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
A CAMP JOKE.

The battalion left Warrington on Sunday, and a large number of spectators assembled to give us a good send-off. Two special trains conveyed us to Denbigh, 'where we arrived, after nearly a three-hours' journey. A quarter of an hour's march brought us to the camping ground, and, after the usual drawing of blan- kets, &c., tea was served, and we settled down in our new quarters. There was no parade on Monday before break- fast, and the time .was fully occupied by a large number of, rnen in changing the tents, so as to get- the camp more compact. After breakfast, the battalion marched out of camp in the direc- tion of Bettws-y-Coed, and were exercised in company drill under the respective company officers. The new despatch-riding section, which consists of the old signallers, Scouts and cyclists, were fully instructed in their new ilutie by. Lieulonants Seyrnour-doncs d Raertson. The afternoon was free, will; the exception of rifle inspection, and the majority of us left cam,p for the village. Some very interesting old ruined castles are to be seen round here, and the stories attached to these are innumerable. One is to the effect that Queen' Boadicea is buried in a stone coffin on Newmarket Hill, and on a visitor asking a guide how they knew it was Queen Boadicea, that worthy replied, " Oh ! we bored to see 'Cr." N.B. : This is one of our camp jokes.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERESTING PRESENTATION.

At an early hour on Tuesday morning we were on parade, which consisted of company drill, and the opportunity was taken in rehears- ing marching on the markers by the roll of the drums, instead of verbal command. After breakfast, the battalion were exercised in skir- mishing. The despatch riders indulged in a little scheme of their own, which was very well carried out.

Before the battalion marched away from parade on Tuesday morning, Quartermaster. Sergeant Jones was presented with the T.F. efficiency medal.

An ea-rly visit was paid by General Lindsay, commanding the West Lancashire Territorials, In order to inspect the lines of the Warrington Army Service Corps. The general service wag- gonS were drilling, doing what the men gener- ally. "playing circus," under Sergeant- Major Nicholls. Other units were on parade as the General, with Major Shobridge, D. 5.0., his new chief of staff, walked through the lines. The General was satisfied with what he saw, and paid a tribute to the work of the Warring- ton lads.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0292.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL IN SUMMER.

The first matches in the battalion inter-com- pany football competition were played on Tues- day evening. To avoid drawn- games, corners are counted, four corners equalling one goal. "A" Company beat "G". Company by two goals Iwo corners to one corner, and "F" Company settled the aspirations of " B " Company by two goals one corner to two goals. A concert was given in the Drill Hall, Den. bight later in the evening, under the presidency of the Major, for the benefit of the froops in camp. Several local artiste' gave their ser- vices, and their efforts were appreciated by a. good attendance of the "Terriers." The con- cert had been arranged by the local Reception Committee. Major APpleton voiced the thanks of' the men.

The early parade on Wednesday was held on the battalion parade ground, and firing exer- cises were thoroughly gone into under the com- pany officers. 'After breakfast the Warrington men were marched to a different part of the inanceuvre area, and under Majors Appleton and Crosfield, went through skirmishing prac- tices in the direction of Ruthin.

In the early mornings there is heavy dew on the grass in camp, so the troops are taken along the roads for a route march to get their circu- lation going before breakfast. The general trouble early on is to. get ,warm, whilst after breakfast everybody is doing his best to keep cool.

The frosty cold at nights are trying the men somewhat, and one recruit from Warrington staggered a sergeant by asking him how many degrees below zero the thermometer had to fall before they would be allotted extra blankets. Cold though it has proved, the conditions have not yet become arctic enough for extra blan- kets. At least regulations. imply so.

===== 0002601_19130823_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
NIGHT MAN(EUVRES.

"A " COmpany fairly ran through "H " Com- pany in the football competition, winning by two goals 6 corners to three corners. The des- patch riders turned out for a, night scheme on Wednesday, and did some very interesting scouting work. A number of officers. and N.C.O.'s of .the 'battalion witnessea the opera- tions,. and expressed themselves pleased with the movements.

Thursday. On Thursday morning the battalion moved out of camp:, marched about three and a half miles,, and at \Vern engaged in skirmishing and general- instructions.

The- weather yesterday (Thursday) was rather dull and threatening.. The health of the men is, on the whole, Vc,ry good, although Surgeon-Major Adams seems very busy with his patients each morning and evening.

The -Conduct of the whole camp is very satis- factory, and the inhabitants of Denbigh seem very pleased with the behaviour of the troops.

HONOUR FOR WARRINGTON MINISTER. Friday. In the inter-football competition " E " Com- pany beat the Despatch Riders by one goal three corners to a corner. The preliminary heats for the 100 yards and half-mile were run off on Thursday, and four men were selected to represent the battalion in the brigade sports 10-morrow (Saturday).

The Rev. W. Bracecamp, who, by the way, has been promoted to third , class chaplain franking as major), has arrived in camp.. A fine Performance was given by the 4th South Lancashire. Band in the Market Square, Denbigh, before a large and appreciative audi4 ence on Thursthiy.

The battalion was early astir and breakfast was served at six -o'clock. At seven the men marched out to perform the field firing exer- cises.. Haversack rations' were taken by 'each man and the battalion returned in the after- noon. The despatch riding section paraded for a very interesting scheme of their own. under Lieutenants Seymour-Jones and Robert- son.

The Y.M.C.A. are doing their usual good work in their marquees. Writing Materials, &e.. are provided, free •of charge, to the men, and nightly concerts are • arranged. THE REcaurs QUAINT ANSWER.

This afternoon we had a heavy rainfall ; quite a change to the sweltering weather of the past week. General Mackinnon, the commander of the district, paid us a visit and was an inter- ested spectator of the Warrington men's Shoot- ing. Later in the afternoon he visited the
